The editors and the tree follow as they do for a deletion in the Explorer. `ExtensionTrashUnavailable` (a scope with no trash) makes the discard delete as `git.discardUntrackedChangesToTrash` off does; the first question said "Move to Trash", which is a small lie in a host that has no trash and no user (nothing shows dialogs there).
543. **An intent-to-add file is discarded like an untracked one.** VS Code treats `git add -N` files as tracked and runs `git checkout -- <file>`, which restores the empty blob the index holds: the user's text is lost silently. Emetos asks "Are you sure you want to DELETE the following untracked file", moves the file to the trash (or `git rm -f`s it) and forgets the entry with `git rm --cached`. `git.cleanAllTracked` leaves these files, `git.cleanAllUntracked` takes them.
544. **Renames are staged and unstaged as pairs.** Staging a rename that is only an intent to add names the old and the new path (`git add -A -- old new`); unstaging a staged rename names both (`git reset -q HEAD -- old new`). VS Code unstages the new path only, which leaves a staged deletion of the old file and an untracked new one. A file that is both renamed in the index and changed in the work tree stages only its own path (the old one is not in the index any more, and git refuses a path it does not know).
545. **Every path is literal** (`GIT_LITERAL_PATHSPECS=1`) for the commands of this step. VS Code passes the paths as pathspecs, so a file called `a[1].go` or `*.txt` is a pattern for it; with 5,000 paths git also reads them from `--pathspec-from-file` where it can (the runner's rule from step 24.4), and the literal flag applies to that too.
546. **`git.confirmCommittedDelete` is acted on.** VS Code 1.143 declares it ("Confirm before deleting committed files with Git.", true) and never reads it: `git.delete` runs `git rm` at once. The SPEC and the step list the confirmation, so `git.delete` asks "Are you sure you want to delete 'a.go'?" (**Delete File**) unless the setting is off.
547. **Unstage and Discard from the editor find the file's resource in the right group.** VS Code's `getSCMResource` returns the work tree resource, then the staged one, then the merge one, so Unstage from the editor of a file that is in both groups does nothing; here Unstage looks in the staged group and Stage and Discard in the work tree groups.
548. **`git.stageAll` stages only tracked files outside the "mixed" mode.** VS Code passes the paths of the work tree and untracked groups to `git add -u`, which a git of today refuses for an untracked path ("pathspec did not match any file(s) known to git"). Emetos gives `-A` the work tree and untracked groups with `mixed` and `-u` only the tracked files otherwise.
549. **`git.ignore` writes the file with plain file I/O**, not through an editor and a `WorkspaceEdit` as VS Code does (which opens and saves `.gitignore`), and escapes the characters git reads as a pattern (`\`, `[`, `*`, `?`, a leading `#` or `!`, trailing spaces). VS Code's escape turns a backslash into a slash for Windows paths, which on Linux would change a file name that has one. A `.gitignore` that is open with unsaved changes is changed on disk and the window asks about it as for any change from outside.
550. **A discard does not ask the editor to reload.** The SPEC said "also asks the editor to reload a clean document"; the window's watcher already does it for any change on disk (§6.3 "External changes": a clean editor reloads, a dirty one asks Compare, Overwrite and Revert), and the extension API has no reload call. `TestAGitDiscardIsAnExternalChangeTheOpenEditorFollows` runs a real `git checkout` and delivers the watcher's event to a window with an open editor. The SPEC text was changed.
551. **Unstage All also forgets intent-to-add files** (`git reset -q HEAD -- .` turns them back into untracked files), as VS Code's does; the files stay on disk.
552. **`git.rename` has no selection of the base name** (VS Code's `valueSelection`): the input box of the extension API has none; the caret is at the end of the path.
553. **`gitscm.DiscardDialogs`** is exported so that the golden test of the window (`internal/gui/app`) draws the same questions the extension asks; it builds the questions from names and a kind and nothing else.
554. **The stage and unstage items of the editor title are not built here.** The step lists the three menus of the Source Control view; VS Code's `editor/title` items for a text editor and for the right side of a diff editor need `isInDiffRightEditor` and `isEmbeddedDiffEditor`, which step 24.12 adds with the diff editor's gutter. `git.activeResourceHasStagedChanges` and `git.activeResourceHasUnstagedChanges` (24.7) are ready for them.
555. **A merge file is "resolved" when it has no conflict marker line**, VS Code's rule (`^<{7}\s|^={7}$|^>{7}\s`, read from the file on a goroutine, a line of at most 16 MiB); a file that cannot be read has none. Every other conflict kind (added by us, added by them, both deleted) asks, as VS Code's "unresolved". The answers about a deletion are done one after the other and a Cancel stops the rest; the files answered before it are done.
556. **VS Code's tests**: `stackOperations.test.ts` is about rebase, push leases and CAS updates of branches (steps 24.13 to 24.15) and `git.test.ts` has no case for the commands of this step: 0 cases ported, 0 skipped. The tests are written from `commands.ts` and `repository.ts`: tables of the starting state against real repositories (modified, deleted, untracked, renamed, intent to add, conflicted, a repository before its first commit, a submodule, spaces and non-ASCII characters, glob characters, a symbolic link, 5,000 paths).

## 2026-10-31: step 24.17, the multi-file diff editor (decisions 560 to 571; decided by the agent, to be reviewed by the user)

560. **A saved multi-file diff editor comes back, unlike a saved diff of provided documents (decision 522).** The step asked for a design: the saved input holds only the kind of its provider, the key of its set and its title (`editorgroup.MultiDiffInput`); never the files, never the URIs of provided documents. The restored tab shows "Waiting for the extension that provides these changes..." and asks the provider of the kind for the files when the extension registers it (`ProvideMultiDiff` makes every pane that waits for that kind ask), so nothing has to be restored before the extensions start. Decision 522 stands for a diff of two documents: its two URIs are all it saves and the provider's documents are not there when it is restored.
561. **`MultiDiffProvider{Kind, Resources(ctx, key)}` is the contract** (VS Code gives the editor a fixed list of resources and a source URI, `multidiff:` plus a hash, from which the workbench asks its `IMultiDiffSourceResolver`; the provider with a kind and a key is that resolver). A set of a provider is named by Kind and Key (`MultiDiffSpec` with both); a fixed set (no Kind) is shown but is not saved. `MultiDiffRegistration.Changed(keys...)` is VS Code's `onDidChange`: every open editor of those keys asks again, off the UI thread, and keeps the folded state and the measured height of the sections of the files that are still there (a section is known by its path and its two documents).
562. **An error of the provider with the tag `MultiDiffGone` closes the tab without a word** (a repository that was closed, a commit that is gone), as a diff of a repository that is gone is dropped (decision 522); any other error is shown in the tab. The tag is a constant of the contract (`extension.TagMultiDiffGone`) that an extension passes to its own error; the window finds it with `errors.Is`.
563. **Only the sections near the viewport have a body.** The layout (`internal/gui/multidiff.Model`) keeps the top of every section from prefix sums, an estimated body height for a section that was never built and the measured one once it was; a section is built when it touches the viewport widened by 300 px above and below, and its body (the editors, tokenizers and open documents of both sides) is ended when it leaves that range, its height kept. When a section above the viewport changes height the section at the top stays where it is (the scroll offset moves by the difference), which VS Code's virtualized item manager does too. 2,000 files hold the bodies of at most about 40 sections whatever the scroll position (the test measures the sections inside the widened viewport and checks the number of bodies); the headers of all files are cheap and are not built outside the range either.
564. **The body is the diff editor of §9.11 with its own scrolling off**, sized to its content (`ScrollBeyondLastLine` off): the editor passes the wheel on when its scroll extent is 0, so one wheel moves the whole column. A file that cannot be shown as text (a binary file, a file over the limit, a provider that fails for the file) is a notice of 44 px in the body. `diffEditor.hideUnchangedRegions` is not built in the bodies yet (a body shows the whole file); VS Code's multi-file editor turns it on. The scroll position and the folded files are not saved with the window.
565. **The tab is named "<title> (N files)"** (VS Code: "Changes (N files)" through the label of the input), the number following the files the provider made last. The title bar of the editor has Go to Previous Change, Go to Next Change and Collapse All Diffs, which Expand All Diffs replaces while every file is folded (`multiDiffEditorAllCollapsed`); Alt+F5 and Shift+Alt+F5 go to the next and the previous change and cross from file to file when the multi-file editor is the active editor.
566. **The headers are a keyboard list.** Up and Down move between the files (the focused header scrolls into view), Home and End go to the first and the last, Enter and Space fold or unfold, Left folds and Right unfolds, Page Up and Page Down scroll a viewport. VS Code has no keys for the headers; the step asked for navigation between files and changes.
567. **The file actions are a menu, `MenuMultiDiffFileToolbar`** (VS Code's `MultiDiffEditorFileToolbar`, `multiDiffEditor/resource/title`), drawn from the registry in the header of each file with the file's own context keys (`MultiDiffResource.Context`: for Git `scmProvider` and `scmResourceGroup`) and run with the file's `Args`, which for Git has the shape of `SCMArgs` (`{sourceControl, group, uri}`), so a command written for the Source Control rows (`git.stageFile`, `git.unstageFile`) also serves the header. The Git items are `git.stageFile` for the work tree and the untracked group and `git.unstageFile` for the index. The commands are the ones of step 24.8; `uriArg` of the Git extension reads the list of one object that a file's `Args` holds (it read only a string or an object before), so the action finds the file, and a test runs both commands with the `Args` the provider makes and checks the index of a real repository.
568. **The keys of Git's sets**: `group/<repository id>/<group>` (`merge`, `index`, `workingTree`, `untracked` and `all`) and `commit/<repository id>/<hash>`. `all` is the work tree and the untracked files of the repository, which is what Open All Changes opened before (decision 526 had it open one tab for each file and ask above 10; the SPEC said the multi-file editor and the wording of decision 526 is replaced: Open All Changes now opens one multi-file editor and never asks, since a section costs nothing until it is near the viewport). A kept tab is the default, a preview with the `preview` of the spec.
569. **Open Commit is against the first parent**; a root commit is against the empty tree (`git hash-object -t tree` of nothing, so the hash algorithm of the repository decides), a merge commit against its first parent as `git show --first-parent`, with renames found at 50 % (`--find-renames=50%`, VS Code's `diffBetween` threshold) and the files sorted by path. The argument is a revision, or an object with `id` (also `historyItemId` or `hash`, the names the Graph and the Timeline use) and `revealUri`, the file to scroll to. A revision that starts with `-` is refused (`GitRevisionInvalid`) and one that names no commit fails as git fails (`GitCommitNotFound` for a range that holds none).
570. **A scope without a workbench is not warned about**: when `Documents().ProvideMultiDiff` fails with `ExtensionDocumentUnavailable`, the Git extension logs nothing and goes on without the multi-file editor (as decision 536 for the documents of versions and 24.6 for the decorations), so a host without a workbench, and the hostOver tests of `cmd/emetos`, see no log line.
571. **Three theme colors**, from VS Code: `multiDiffEditor.headerBackground`, `multiDiffEditor.background` and `multiDiffEditor.border` (`kit.Colors.MultiDiffHeaderBackground`, `MultiDiffBackground`, `MultiDiffBorder`).
572. **A header's file icon is asked of the icon theme as the Explorer asks it**: with the name, the name of the folder and the language of the file (`WidgetOpts.Language`, the window's language detection on the path of the header), so that a Go file has the Go icon and `notes.md` the Markdown one; the first version asked with the name only and a theme gives most icons by language. A test covers a `.go` and a `.md` file, and the goldens `multi_diff_{dark,light}.png` show the icons.
573. **An added file shows a red line 1 on the left and a deleted file a green line 1 on the right, as VS Code does.** Checked against the pinned VS Code sources: the multi-file editor gives a missing side an empty synthetic document (`multiDiffEditorViewModel.ts`, `createSyntheticDocument('', null)`), and the diff of an empty document against a text is the single change of line 1 to all the lines (`diffProviderFactoryService.ts`, "the original file is empty": `LineRange(1, 2)` against `LineRange(1, n + 1)`), which `diffEditorDecorations.ts` draws as a whole-line delete decoration on line 1 of the original. `internal/core/diff` (`computeDiff`) and the decorations of the diff editor of §9.11 are ports of those, so nothing differs and nothing was changed.

## 2026-10-31: step 24.22, blame (decisions 600 to 608; decided by the agent, to be reviewed by the user)

600. **Blame blames the version in HEAD and maps the document to it with a line diff; the step says "the saved file" and "edits mapped through the document's changes".** The cache is by file and HEAD (the step says so too), and a work tree blame changes whenever the file does, so it cannot be cached by HEAD. VS Code blames HEAD as well (`blame2(path, HEAD)`) and maps with the diff information of the editor. Emetos has no such information for a document that is not a diff or a quick diff original (the quick diff arrives in 24.11), and a history of the edits is wrong when the window starts with a document that is dirty already; a line diff (hashes of the lines, Myers's algorithm, at most 500 lines of difference) of the document against the version in HEAD is right at any time and costs a few milliseconds for the edits of a typing pause. Seeded random edits (`TestMapLinesKeepsEveryUnchangedLine`) check that the lines that were not touched keep their commit. A line that is saved but not committed is uncommitted too (VS Code's "Not Committed Yet"), and a document that differs from HEAD by more than 500 lines has no blame text.
601. **An uncommitted line shows "Uncommitted changes" with the name from `user.name`, not VS Code's "Not Committed Yet".** The step says so ("Uncommitted changes" with the user's name), the SPEC's "You • Uncommitted changes" is the same idea; the text goes through the template (`${subject}` is "Uncommitted changes", `${authorName}` the name, `${authorDateAgo}` "now"), so the user's template applies to it. "You" is the name when git has none. VS Code's separate "Not Committed Yet (Staged)" is not built: a staged change is not told from an unstaged one. An uncommitted line has no hover and its status bar item has no click.
602. **The hover of the blame text has no `$(icon)` names and no Settings gear.** The Markdown renderer of the hover does not draw `$(account)`, `$(history)` or `$(git-commit)` (VS Code's `supportThemeIcons`), so the hover is written without them: the author in bold with an e-mail link, the time, the message, the statistics in plain text and the two links the step names, **Open Commit** and **Copy Hash**. The gear (`workbench.action.openSettings`) is a command of the core, which the hover does not run (decision 603). The avatar and the remote commands of VS Code's hover (GitHub) are not built (D33).
603. **A `command:` link in a hover runs only the commands of the extension that made the hover** (declared by its manifest, or registered by it), and nothing else: not a command of another extension, not one of the core, not another scheme (`ExtensionCommandLinkInvalid` is logged for a malformed one). VS Code's `isTrusted: {enabledCommands}` names the commands; the compiled-in extensions are trusted, and this is the narrowest rule that serves the step (SPEC §10.30, D34). Arguments are VS Code's: a JSON array in the query, percent-encoded.
604. **Open Commit passes `{"id": hash, "revealUri": uri, "root": repository}` to `git.viewCommit`** (step 24.17's command): it opens the multi-file diff editor of the commit, scrolled to the file of the blame (`revealUri`), in the repository named by `root`, so that no question is asked when the window has several. The step 24.17 handler reads all three. A hover link and the status bar click run the same command.
605. **`git.copyContentToClipboard` is registered by the extension with `Commands().Register`, not declared in the manifest.** VS Code has it as a hidden command (it is not in `package.json`); a command with no title is not in the palette. It copies a string argument to the clipboard and does nothing for any other.
606. **A line annotation is 50 pixels from the end of the text and follows the edits as a decoration, until the extension sets new ones.** VS Code's margin is 50px (`margin: '0 0 0 50px'`); a second annotation of a line (two sets of two extensions) is 12 pixels after the first. The decoration has the stickiness that keeps it on its line when Enter is pressed at the end of the line; when a line is deleted its annotation goes to the line that takes its place until the next `Set`, which the blame sends 150 ms after the edit.
607. **Blame starts no timer when there is nothing to show, and nothing runs by itself.** The wait of 150 ms starts only when a text editor is active and at least one of the two texts is on; HEAD moving and a setting changing are the only other starts. The Git extension's tests that count the pending timers of the status debounce are not disturbed by it, and `TestBlameIsIdleWithoutAChangeOfTheCursor` checks that fifty seconds of the fake clock start nothing.
608. **The blame text is the extension's `Set` for the active editor only, one document at a time.** The key `git.blame` holds one document: moving to another editor replaces the set, so no stale text stays in an editor that is not active (VS Code clears the other visible editors by hand). Two editors of the active document (two groups) both show it.

## 2026-10-31: step 24.9, passwords and editors: the helper (decisions 620 to 632; decided by the agent, to be reviewed by the user)

620. **`git.fetch` is built here in its smallest form** (`Repo.Fetch`, the command with no variant, `Retry` on an authentication failure). The step's proof runs `git.fetch` and the tests need a command that makes git ask; the prune, all remotes, ref, progress, autofetch and sync belong to step 24.15, which replaces the handler. `clone` and `push` are tested through the runner with the same helper, since their commands are 24.15 and 24.16.
621. **The variables of git's programs are given per command, not at activation, and only to commands that can ask.** `git.Config.ExtraFor(name, background)` returns them for fetch, pull, push, clone, ls-remote, remote, submodule, checkout, switch, commit, merge, rebase, cherry-pick, revert, tag, am and stash that are not background commands, and the first such command starts the server. The SPEC said "every command"; a status never prompts, and the lazy start keeps every test and every window tab that never fetches from creating a socket and a folder in the runtime folder.
622. **`GIT_EDITOR` follows `git.useEditorAsCommitInput` and the askpass variables follow `git.useIntegratedAskPass`**, VS Code's rule (`gitEditor.ts`, `askpass.ts`). The SPEC's environment bullet said `GIT_EDITOR` is set when `git.useIntegratedAskPass` is true; the sentence in "Passwords and editors" and the setting's description say otherwise, so the setting that is named for the editor decides.
623. **`helper.Main` has a fifth parameter, stderr** (`Main(args, env, stdin, stdout, stderr)`): the step listed four, but stdout is what git reads as the answer, so an explanation of a failure ("missing or invalid credentials") has to go somewhere else. The request also carries `dir`, the working directory of the client, because git runs the editor with a path relative to the repository.
624. **The answer of the editor is 0 when the file was saved while its tab was open.** The window's close event carries no word about unsaved changes, so a file that was saved and then closed with Don't Save counts as saved (what is on disk is what git reads). Closing a tab that was never saved is 1 (git aborts), the Accept title action on a tab with nothing to save is 0, Cancel is always 1.
625. **A cancelled ssh prompt stays `PermissionDenied`**; only the HTTPS prompt (git's "could not read Username/Password for '...': terminal prompts disabled") is `AuthenticationFailed` with Retry. ssh's text after a cancelled passphrase is "Permission denied (publickey)", and telling the two apart would need a link between the prompt and the command (a run id in the environment) that nothing else needs yet.
626. **The extension contract grew by `InputBoxOptions.Message` and `.IgnoreFocusOut`, `QuickPickOptions.IgnoreFocusOut` and `Documents().CloseTabsAs`** (`TabCloseAsk`, `TabCloseSave`, `TabCloseDiscard`). The step said the box has "ignoreFocusOut" and a prompt line "Git: https://host" over the placeholder; the quick input had neither, and a prompt that vanishes when the user clicks away would fail the command. `workbench.quickOpen.closeOnFocusLost` is overridden only while the box asks to stay. Accept and Cancel need to save first and to close without the Save question; `CloseTabs` could not.
627. **`extensions/gitscm/githttp` is new**: `gittest.HTTPBackend` is a wrapper over it, so that the dev command `dev.git.testserver` (which must be in the editor, in debug builds) does not import the test harness or `net/http/httptest`. It listens on 127.0.0.1 only.
628. **The title actions of a commit message keep VS Code's titles** ("Commit" and "Cancel", the SPEC called the first "Accept Commit Message") and both are in the navigation group with the icons `check` and `close`, because the editor title draws only the items with an icon as buttons and VS Code's `secondary` group would put Cancel in the overflow menu. `git.commitMessageAccept` / `Discard` stay out of the palette (`when: false`, VS Code's).
629. **The proof adds a `dev.quickInput.type` step.** The step's command list opens the box but types nothing, and the image is to show "the dots of a password"; `dev.quickInput.type` (new, debug and `emetos_dev` builds, SPEC §15.2) types into the quick input that is shown. Stale folders are found by their pid alone (a folder of a live process stays even if the pid was reused by another program; it goes when that process ends).
630. **The tests have no sshd and no ssh.** The ssh prompts are checked in two ways: `helper.ParsePrompt` against a table of the texts OpenSSH prints (copied from its source and documentation), and the real scripts run by the test with `SSH_ASKPASS_PROMPT` set, as ssh would, through the real server and the boxes of the fake API. `TestMain` of `extensions/gitscm` makes the test binary answer the three flags, which is what `cmd/emetos` does (its `helperMain` has a test of its own).
631. **A silent fetch never asks** (`EMETOS_GIT_COMMAND=fetch` with `EMETOS_GIT_FETCH_SILENT` set, VS Code's `VSCODE_GIT_FETCH_SILENT`): the helper refuses before it connects. Nothing sets the variable yet; the autofetch of step 24.15 will.
632. **The window's exit path now shuts the extensions down.** `Workspaces.exitApplication` ran `Extensions.Shutdown` only when it ended the application itself; the path through a workspace's `exit` function (the window's close) skipped it, so no extension's context was cancelled when Emetos quit that way. The helper's socket folder stayed in the runtime folder (the display test found it), so that path now calls `Shutdown` first, and the Git extension also closes its server on `emetos.workspace.closed`, which is delivered synchronously before the deactivation. Stale folders of dead processes are still removed at the next start.

## 2026-10-31: Glutter 1.9.9 (decided by the agent, to be reviewed by the user)
609. **Emetos uses Glutter 1.9.9, and `kit.List` gives the model's heights to `SliverVariedExtentList`; `kit/sliverrows.go` and `docs/GLUTTER-ISSUES.md` are deleted.** Glutter 1.9.9 (its steps 29.40 and 29.41) answers the issue Emetos sent: `RenderSliverVariedExtentList` keeps the extents and their running sums, so the offset of a row is a read, the row at an offset is a binary search, and the scroll extent is exact when the child count is known and the builder gives every extent. `sliverRows`, `renderSliverRows`, `exactExtentDelegate` and the `// Glutter:` comment (decision 462) are removed; the list's item extent builder returns the model's `RowHeightAt(i)` for a row in range and none past the end (which is what makes the extent exact). **The rule of 1.9.9** (the builder gives the same extents until the widget is built again or the viewport extent, the cross axis extent or the child count change; it must not depend on the scroll offset) holds because the builder reads the model's tops, which change only when the model does, and every change of the model (`SetItems`, a filter, `RefreshHeights`) calls `SetState` on the list, so the next build makes a new builder and Glutter drops its sums. A change of the heights that does not notify the model would leave stale sums: `RefreshHeights` is the call for it, as before. The cost is that every build of the list lets Glutter fill the sums once (about 1.7 ms for 100,000 rows in Glutter's benchmark), where the workaround read the tops directly. Measured by `TestHugeChanges` (Source Control, 100,000 files): a jump to the exact end took 9 to 10 ms of processor time before and 10 to 13 ms after, the same within the noise of the machine.

## 2026-10-31: step 24.18 (Stash)

640. **A stash editor is named by the hash of the stash commit, and its kind is `git-stash`.** The multi-file diff editor of a stash is `OpenMultiDiff` with the kind `git-stash` (the provider `stashResources`) and the key `<repository id>/<hash>`; the index of a stash (`stash@{n}`, which VS Code puts in the URI) changes when another stash is made or one is dropped, and a tab must keep showing the stash it was opened for. The tab therefore also survives a pop (VS Code's does, too): the commits are still there, so the provider reads the stash by hash (`Repo.StashChangesAt`) whether or not it is in the list. The editor actions find the stash in the list by that hash at the time they run, and say "This stash is not in the stash list any more." when it is gone. Dropping from the editor (`git.stashDropEditor`) marks the key as dropped and tells the window (`Changed`), the provider answers `MultiDiffGone` and the tab closes, which is VS Code's `closeActiveEditor` after the drop. A closed repository closes its stash tabs the same way. The tab is titled `Git Stash #<index>: <description>` as VS Code titles it.
641. **The `resourceScheme` of a multi-file diff editor of a provider is its kind, and its title commands get `{groupId, kind, key}`.** VS Code's multi-file editor has the resource `multiDiffSourceUri`, whose scheme `git-stash` the `when` of `git.stashApplyEditor`, `git.stashPopEditor` and `git.stashDropEditor` read; the Emetos editor has no document, so the window sets `resourceScheme` to the kind of the provider (`internal/gui/app/exttitle.go`, `groupContext`) and, in place of `uri`, gives the commands the `kind` and the `key` of the set. The `when` of the three items is VS Code's, word for word (`config.git.enabled && !git.missing && resourceScheme == git-stash`); no existing editor kind is changed (a fixed set has no scheme).
642. **Apply, Pop and Drop are three buttons, before the actions of the multi-file editor.** VS Code shows Apply with Pop as its alternative action (Alt) and Drop, at `navigation@1` and `@2`, where the multi-file editor's Previous and Next Change are at `@1` and `@2` too, so VS Code's order between them is the order of registration. Emetos has no alternative actions and the step asks for Apply, Pop and Drop in the title, so they are `navigation@0.1`, `@0.2` and `@0.3` (Codicons `git-stash-apply`, `git-stash-pop`, `trash`) and come first.
643. **`git.stashView`, `git.stashPop`, `git.stashApply` and `git.stashDrop` take `{"index": n}`, and the stash commands take `{"message": "..."}`**, so that a proof, a keybinding or a test can run them without the picker or the input box (VS Code's commands take no arguments here); an index that is not in the list gives "There is no stash #n in the repository.". Without them the commands ask as VS Code does. The step's proof command `["git.stashView"]` would stop at the picker, so the proof used `["git.stashView", {"index": 0}]` after a pause that lets the stash finish.
644. **`Documents().Dirty()` and `Documents().SaveAll(ctx, uris)` are built here**, for `git.promptToSaveFilesBeforeStash`; step 24.10 lists them for `git.promptToSaveFilesBeforeCommit` and uses the same pair. `Dirty` lists the open files (not untitled documents) with unsaved changes, in the order they were opened, on the UI thread; `SaveAll` saves the open ones among the URIs as the Save command does (with its participants), blocks until all are written and so runs off the UI thread (`extensionDocuments.SaveAll` posts the saves to the UI thread and waits), skips a document that is not open or not dirty, and fails with `ExtDocumentNotSaved` for the first one that was not saved, `ExtDocumentSaveUnavailable` for a window tab that is not on screen and `ExtDocumentSaveCancelled` when `ctx` ends first. A scope that cannot show documents has no dirty files and `SaveAll` fails with `ExtensionDocumentUnavailable`. `fakeapi` has `SetDirty`, `SavedAll` and `FailSave`.
645. **The question about unsaved files is VS Code's, logic and quirks included**: with `always` and something staged, only the unsaved files that are staged are asked about (VS Code filters by the index whenever the index is not empty); with `staged` always only those; with `never` none. One file: "The following file has unsaved changes which won't be included in the stash if you proceed: b.txt.\n\nWould you like to save it before stashing?"; several: "There are N unsaved files.\n\nWould you like to save them before stashing?"; the buttons are **Save All & Stash**, **Stash Anyway** and Cancel. When the files cannot be saved nothing is stashed and an error says why (VS Code would let the rejected `save()` end the command): a stash that leaves out the work the user asked to save is not what was chosen.
646. **A stash that conflicts shows a warning and keeps the stash; there is no "Show Changes" button.** VS Code's `StashConflict` message, "There are merge conflicts while applying the stash. Please resolve them before committing your changes.", is a warning (not modal) whose button runs `workbench.view.scm`; an extension cannot run a command of the window, and the merge group of the Source Control view shows the conflicts by itself after the refresh that every command of the user ends with. The warning replaces the generic failure message (`mutation.failed`, a new optional step of `mutation`), the other failures (`LocalChangesOverwritten`, `NoStashFound`, `NoLocalChanges`) are the generic "Git: <message>" with **Show Git Output**.
647. **The changes of a stash are read from its commits, not with `git stash show -u`.** VS Code runs `git stash show stash@{n} --name-status -z -u`; before git 2.32 `-u` of `stash show` meant `--patch`, and Emetos supports git 2.30. `Repo.StashChanges(index)` and `StashChangesAt(hash)` take `git diff --name-status` of the first parent against the stash (renames at 50 %) and the files of the commit that holds the untracked files (the third parent, `git ls-tree -r`), which are the added files of the stash; they show with the letter **U** and the untracked color, where VS Code's editor shows no letters. The untracked file is read from the third parent, the others from the stash commit and the first parent, which is how VS Code's `_viewStash` chooses `modifiedUriRef`. A stash with no change opens nothing and says "The stash has no changes.".
648. **A stash before the first commit is found out before git runs.** VS Code reads "You do not have the initial commit yet" in the error output of `git stash push`; Emetos asks `HasCommit` first (`stashNeedsACommit`, tag `StashNoInitialCommit`) and shows the same information, "The repository does not have any commits. Please make an initial commit before creating a stash.", so no new rule is added to the classification of git's output (SPEC §10.30 "Errors" lists VS Code's codes). `git.stashStaged` on a git older than 2.35 says "Needs git 2.35 or newer" as an error without running git (the palette and the Stash submenu hide it by `gitVersion235`).
649. **The Stash submenu is in the `...` menu of the view and of a repository** (`scm/title` and `scm/repository` at `2_main@6`, `scmProvider == git`), with VS Code's entries and groups (`1_stash` Stash, Stash (Include Untracked), Stash Staged (`gitVersion235`); `2_apply`; `3_pop`; `4_drop`; `5_preview`). `git.repositories.stash` and the artifacts tree are not built (SPEC "Not built").
650. **The picker lists `#<index>: <description>` with "<age> • <branch>"**, the age in VS Code's short words without "ago" (`fromNow` of `util.ts` ported as `fromNowShort`: "now", "5 mins", "2 hrs", "3 days", "1 wk", "2 mos", "1 yr"); a repository with no stash shows the row "$(info) This repository has no stashes.", which does nothing when chosen. `git.useCommitInputAsStashMessage` starts the input box with the text of the commit box; VS Code skips it while the box holds the commit template, which step 24.10 added (decision 682).
651. **A saved Git multi-file editor waits for its repository** (fix of step 24.17). A restored editor asks the provider for its files when the extension starts, before discovery has found the repositories, and the provider answered `MultiDiffGone`, which closes the tab: a Git editor did not survive a restart. `multiDiffResources` (groups and commits) and `stashResources` now call `awaitRepository`, which waits off the UI thread, until `ctx` ends, for the repository to have its first status. It answers "gone" at once when the first search ended and the repository is not one that started (`stashState.known`, set when a trusted repository starts and dropped when it closes or fails to open): a folder that is not a repository any more, a repository that is not trusted (the tab is not kept waiting for the question), a closed one. Tests ask before discovery and get the files after it, and cover both ways of being gone.

660. **The Timeline API registers a provider and gets a registration, with no `OnDidChange` event** (`extension/timeline.go`): `RegisterProvider(TimelineProvider{ID, Label, Schemes, Provide})` returns `TimelineRegistration{Changed(uris...), Dispose()}`, as the content and the multi-file providers do (decisions 536 and 560). `Changed` with no URI means every file; the view always loads the items it shows again, so VS Code's `reset` flag is not needed. `Limit` is an int, 0 for all (VS Code's `undefined`); VS Code's `{timestamp, id}` limit form is not used.
661. **An item carries `Args`, and the arguments of its commands are one list** (`extension.TimelineArgs`): VS Code gives a command the `TimelineItem` object its provider made (the extension host keeps them by handle) and `(item, uri, source, selection)`. Emetos gives `[{"source", "id", "uri", "contextValue", "label", "args"}]`, where `args` is the item's own `Args`, so the extension keeps no table of its items; the Git extension puts there the hashes, the paths and the message it needs. A click on an item whose `Command` has no `Args` runs it with the same list.
662. **A provider ID that is taken fails (`ExtTimelineProviderTaken`)**: VS Code lets a second provider replace the first ("allow any overwriting here", issue 89553) and relies on the extension host to refuse; here the window tab refuses, as for the content schemes (`ExtContentSchemeTaken`).
663. **The service is `internal/core/timeline` and the model of the view is `Pane` in `internal/gui/views/timeline`**, so that the logic of `timelinePane.ts` is tested with fake providers and no widget, and the host binds the service without a view. §5.2 lists the new core package.
664. **Not built in the Timeline**: the selection of several rows and "Compare Selected" (`timeline.compareSelected`: the view selects one row, and `listMultiSelection`, `listDoubleSelection` and `timelineCanCompareSelection` are false; the Git commands of the menu have `!listMultiSelection` in any case), the timeline of a folder and the workspace timeline ("Pin Workspace Timeline"), editable items, "Local History", the command links in the tooltip of a commit (they need the trust rules of §9.12's hover), the avatar and the relative date in the tooltip (the date is written out; a relative one would go stale in a tooltip that is made once).
665. **A provider that fails does not leave "Loading timeline for ..." on the screen**: VS Code refreshes the view after a request only when a refresh is owed, so when the only provider rejects, the loading message stays. The pane refreshes when the last request ends, whatever it gave, and logs the failure once at Warn (a cancelled request is not logged).
666. **The last item of a page is compared with the extra commit that was read to know there is another page**: VS Code takes that commit off the list before it makes the items, so the last item of each page is compared with the empty tree and its diff shows the whole file as added. Here the item of a page boundary is compared with the commit after it, as the others are.
667. **The cursor of the Git timeline is the commit and the path the file had in it** (`<hash>:<path>`): VS Code starts the next page with `git log <hash> --follow -- <current path>`, which finds nothing when the page starts before a rename (the current name does not exist in those commits), so the history seems to end at the rename. The path comes from `--numstat` (`Commit.FilePath`), and the diff of an item compares the file under the name it had in each commit, which also fixes the diff of the rename commit itself (VS Code compares the new name with the old commit).
668. **The short hash in the titles of diffs is `git.commitShortHashLength` (7)**, not the 8 characters VS Code's timeline cuts: the spec's titles are `a.go (abc1234) ↔ a.go (def5678)` and the Graph and Open Commit already use the setting.
669. **The staged item is dated by the index file and the uncommitted item by the file**: VS Code dates them with the time of the last operation of the repository run by the extension, and with "now". The time the files were last written is true whoever wrote them (a terminal too) and does not change while the view is open. Tests set the time with `os.Chtimes`.
670. **The description of a view's header ("a.go", "a.go (pinned)") shows only while the view is open** (`SideBarViewsOpts.Description`); VS Code's `.description` is hidden in a collapsed pane header.
671. **The Git extension registers its timeline provider when the first repository opens**, as VS Code does, so the filter of the view lists no "Git History" without a repository; a host with no Timeline view (`ExtensionTimelineUnavailable`) logs nothing, as decisions 536, 570 and the decorations.
672. **`LogFile` asks for `--diff-merges=first-parent` from git 2.31**: without it `--numstat` has nothing for a merge commit, which then has no path and no stat. With it `git log --follow` lists the merge commits that changed the file (it lists none without the option); on git 2.30 (the minimum) a log that follows renames leaves them out.
673. **The title buttons of the view are built by the window, not from a menu of the registry**: the pin button changes its icon (`pin`, `pinned`) and its title with the state, which a command has one icon for. Extensions can not add to the title (VS Code has `MenuId.TimelineTitle` for its own actions only). The filter menu has a row for each source, checked while it is shown.
674. **`timeline.focus` and `timeline.refresh` are in the palette; `timeline.toggleFollowActiveEditor` and `files.openTimeline` are not**, as in VS Code (a button and a menu row). Open Timeline is in the menu of the Explorer for a file (VS Code also for a folder) and, as the spec says, in the menu of a tab for a file.
675. **The filter of the sources is saved with the window** (`WindowState.Timeline`, `{"excluded": [...]}`), where VS Code keeps `timeline.excludeSources` in the profile's storage; Emetos has no such store, and the window's state is where the folds of the Source Control view are kept too. A source that is not registered any more stays in the filter until the user changes it, as in VS Code.
680. **One flow for the twenty commit commands, in VS Code's order** (`commitRun`, `commit.go`): unsaved files, smart commit, the flags, an empty commit, no-verify, the message, the diagnostics, the protected branch, then the commit. Each question is a step that goes on or stops. VS Code's texts and buttons are kept (the SPEC's paraphrase "Save All & Commit / Commit Staged Changes" is corrected to **Save All & Commit Changes** / **Commit Changes**); the modal messages of VS Code are a message and a detail here, as the dialogs of 24.8 are. With the suggestion of smart commit off and nothing staged a commit does nothing, with no word, as in VS Code. The information "There are no changes to commit." has **Create Empty Commit** as a button of the notification: closing the notification stops the commit.
681. **A commit that arrives while another runs waits for it and for the status it leaves.** VS Code's operations queue does the same, but its second command has already decided from the old state; here the second command starts its questions again once the first ended and the status is in (`commitQueue`, `endCommit`), so "nothing is staged any more" is what it sees. The box and the button are disabled while a commit runs, and `git.commitInProgress` is set (it was always false).
682. **The commit template is applied by the extension, not by a new API.** VS Code's `SourceControl.commitTemplate` makes the workbench fill an empty box (or one that still holds the old template). Emetos's contract has no such property, so the extension reads the template (`commit.template`, `MERGE_MSG`, `SQUASH_MSG`) with the first status and again when the commit or the merge, rebase or cherry-pick state changes, and sets the box with `SCMInput.SetValue` under the same rule. The difference: a value set through `SetValue` goes into the history of the box (Up shows the template), where the workbench's own fill does not. A change of `commit.template` is seen at the next commit, merge or `git.restoreCommitTemplate`, not at every refresh (the read costs a process).
683. **A commit message tab closed without saving is "Commit operation was cancelled."** The editor of 24.9 answers 1 for a tab closed without saving, so git says "there was a problem with the editor" (lower case since git 2.4x) and exits 1; VS Code's editor always answers 0 and git then finds an empty message. The user who pressed Cancel or closed the tab gets an information, not an error. A message saved empty is VS Code's "Commit operation was cancelled due to empty commit message.".
684. **The guided author setting** (`identity.go`): VS Code only says "Make sure you configure your "user.name" and "user.email" in git." with a link. Emetos adds **Set Name and E-mail...**: it asks for what git lacks (the name and the e-mail, or one of them), shows the commands (`git config --global user.name 'Ada'`, quoted for a shell) in a dialog and runs them only after **Run the Commands**; it runs `git config --global -- user.name <value>` so that a value that starts with a dash is a value; the information afterwards has **Commit Again**. VS Code's "Learn More" button (a web page) is not built. When git lacks both, it names the e-mail first, so `NoUserEmailConfigured` is the error and both questions are asked.
685. **The diagnostics hook needs two additions to `api.Problems()`**: `Read(uri)` (every owner's markers of a file, VS Code's `languages.getDiagnostics`) and `Show()` (the **View Problems** button, which VS Code does with `workbench.panel.markers.view.focus`; extensions cannot run host commands). A window with no Problems panel (a headless host) commits without a word: the errors `ExtensionProblemsUnavailable` and `ExtensionDeactivated` are not logged. Markers with no source are ignored, as VS Code does; a language server that names no source is therefore never a reason to stop a commit.
686. **The remembered post-commit command lasts for the session.** `git.rememberPostCommitCommand` keeps, in VS Code, the choice from the dropdown in the global state of the window across restarts; here it is a field of the repository and ends with it. A file of the state folder would be one more thing to read and to get wrong for a choice the user can make again with one click. Without the setting the choice is forgotten after the commit, as in VS Code. The post-commit command is started and not waited for (VS Code awaits it; `git.push` and `git.sync` are placeholders until 24.15, which can wait for them).
687. **The sync tooltip is VS Code's three texts** (a fix of step 24.5, which had "Pull N and push M commits from/to origin/main" for every case): "Pull N commits from origin/main" (behind only, or a read-only remote), "Push N commits to origin/main" (ahead only), "Pull N and push M commits between origin/main". `refresh_test.go` follows.
688. **Branch protection uses a small glob** (`glob.go`: `*`, `**`, `?`, `[set]`, `{a,b}`, `\`, a leading `!`; a star does not match a leading dot or a slash) instead of picomatch, which is a JavaScript package. The prompt for the name of the new branch is a smaller `promptForBranchName` than VS Code's (the prefix, the white space character, the validation regex and the existing branches are honoured; the random names and the regenerate button are not), marked `TODO(step 24.13)` for the branch commands to share. The Commit submenu has no **Abort Rebase** entry until 24.14 brings the command.
689. **`git.commit` while a rebase is stopped runs `git rebase --continue`** (VS Code's `repository.commit` does; the button says **Continue**). Step 24.14 is to add what the rest of the state needs (the box holding the message of the stopped commit, its warning); this step adds the button states, the routing and a test of a stopped rebase that is continued.
690. **Signing is passed to git.** `git.enableCommitSigning` gives `-S` and nothing else gives a flag (`commit.gpgsign` of the configuration decides); Emetos never creates, reads or asks for a key. `git.addAICoAuthor` is registered and does nothing: Emetos has no AI edits to find (SPEC "Not built").
700. **The stripe sits right of the folding controls, as VS Code's does; the SPEC's "at the left of the line numbers' gutter" was a loose phrase and is corrected (step 24.11).** VS Code gives the stripe a 5 px margin in the line decorations column, between the line numbers and the text, with the folding chevron left of it (in Emetos the chevron takes the first 16 px of that column, so the stripe is 5 px after them). A width of 5 px fits the 10 px of `editor.lineDecorationsWidth`; a narrower column lets the stripe reach the text, which VS Code does too.
701. **A provider's `Original` returns the URI of a provided document, and the host reads the text and keeps it until told.** VS Code's `provideOriginalResource` returns a `git:` URI and its model keeps the text model of it, updated by the content provider's change event; the contract here is the same shape (a URI, read through `Documents().ProvideContent`'s registry), plus `SourceControl.RefreshQuickDiff()` for "the originals may have changed" because a document that no editor has open is never told `ContentRegistration.Changed`. Typing never asks a provider or reads an original again; only a provider added or removed, a Refresh or a Save As does.
702. **The Git extension tells the host after a status only when what the providers answer from changed.** The signature is the HEAD commit, the size and time of `.git/index` and the files and statuses of the four groups. The index file is in it because staging a second part of a file that is staged already changes no status but changes the original of the Working Tree provider. A refresh of an unchanged state costs the host nothing; the alternative (a Refresh after every status) would run `git ls-files` for every open file after every burst of file changes of a build.
703. **"Tracked" is `git ls-files`, not `git check-ignore`.** VS Code's provider tests untracked (the groups) and ignored (`check-ignore`); a tracked file that a pattern of `.gitignore` matches is not reported ignored by git, so it keeps its marks, and an untracked file that is neither in a group (`git.untrackedChanges: hidden`) nor ignored has no original (in VS Code the read of its missing index version fails and the model has no diff). One `ls-files` answers all three. It is the one process an `Original` costs; the cheap rules (a link, `.git`, the groups, the owner) run first.
704. **The next and previous change commands work on the editor's marks, not on the model's last result.** VS Code's `GotoNextChangeAction` reads `quickDiffModel.changes`, which are for the text of the last comparison; the editor's marks are decorations that follow the edits, so the commands are right in the 250 ms before a new comparison. The context key `quickDiffDecorationCount` is the editor's too (VS Code's is the workbench's for the active editor). The key `Alt+F5` is the editor's (weight 100) and the diff editor keeps its own (weight 200, `textCompareEditorVisible`), as VS Code has them.
705. **Each editor of a file has its own model.** VS Code shares one `QuickDiffModel` per file through a reference collection; here a file open in two groups is compared twice (two goroutines, two `git ls-files` after a refresh). The shared model is a small change when it matters (the model holds no editor), and was left out because the editors do not share their other background work either (the tokens and the folding are per editor).
706. **No quick diff in the editors of a diff, a merge or a peek.** VS Code hides the gutter glyphs in the modified editor of an inline diff and builds no model for documents that are not text files; those editors here are made with `newTextEditorWith` and an `adjust` function, which is what the window looks at. The overview ruler and the minimap of such an editor show nothing of it either.
707. **Not built from VS Code's quick diff:** the toggle of each provider's visibility (`workbench.scm.action.toggleQuickDiffVisibility.<id>`, the menu `SCMQuickDiffDecorations` and the state key `workbench.scm.quickDiffProviders.hidden`), the `contributed` kind (providers of the `quickDiffProvider` of a language selector), the fade of 0.5 s of a stripe that appears on hover and the `80 ms` transitions of the stripe and the triangle. `scm.diffDecorationsGutterAction` acts since step 24.12 (see 721).
708. **The pattern is drawn pixel by pixel.** VS Code's pattern is a CSS background (a gradient on a `width` x `width` tile, repeated down the stripe); here each pixel of the tile that the gradient colors is a 1 px rectangle repeated down the run of the change (a few rectangles for each `width` px of height), computed from the gradient formula, instead of a tiled paint (not looked for: a tile of 1 to 5 px is cheap this way). The run is one change, not one line, so the tile starts at the top of the change and not at the top of each line.
709. **Format on save of modifications reads the primary original through the same registry** and falls back to the file on disk when no provider has one (a file outside a repository, an untracked file, `git` missing). An original with CRLF line breaks is split by `diff.SplitLines`, so it matches the document's `\n`. The baseline is asked with the deadline of the save: a slow `git` makes the save wait up to `editor.formatOnSaveTimeout`, as a slow formatter does.
720. **The extension gets the changes of two texts from the host (`Documents().LineChanges`).** VS Code's Git extension reads `TextEditor.diffInformation`, which this API does not have, and an extension cannot reach the diff algorithm of the quick diff. The host compares the two texts with that algorithm (time limit 1 s) and returns `LineChange`s; the Git extension uses it to find the lines a selection touches and to cut a change out of the file. White space at the ends of lines always counts here: VS Code's selection commands follow `scm.diffDecorationsIgnoreTrimWhitespace`, so with that setting on a change that is only white space is a change for the staging commands and not a mark in the gutter.
721. **A click on a stripe opens the peek within 3 px of it, and the stripe is looked at before the folding controls.** VS Code's `onEditorMouseUp` takes the gutter target of the line decorations; this editor's stripe is 3 px wide by default, so a hit slack of 3 px each side makes it easy to hit, and the press is claimed when the stripe is on a line with a mark (`startsStripePress`), released elsewhere, so a click on a fold chevron or a line number is not taken. `scm.diffDecorationsGutterAction` `none` leaves every click alone; `diff` is the default.
722. **The peek is a zone with the frame as a border, no arrow.** VS Code draws an arrow from the frame to the line (`arrowSize`) and a 1 px frame; the zone here is `Height + 2 + 6` rows (VS Code's formula) but at most a third of the editor and at least five rows, framed by a top and a bottom border in the color of the change (the stripe's colors), with the right edge short of the scrollbar. The arrow is not drawn.
723. **The peek's small diff editor is the inline diff editor of §9.11 in `Embedded` mode.** VS Code's `EmbeddedDiffEditorWidget` is a diff editor with `renderSideBySide: false`; the model gets `ModelOpts.Embedded`, which makes it inline, counts white space, has no ruler and no glyph margin, and gives the two editors the context keys `isEmbeddedDiffEditor`, `isInDiffRightEditor` and `isInDiffLeftEditor`. The modified side is the document of the outer editor (typing in the peek edits it); the original of a provider that names a file is a read-only document of the peek alone, and a provided document (the index version) is shared through `AdoptVirtual` with the diff editors that show it. The scroll of the change into the middle of the small editor waits for the end of the frame (`binding.Post`): the editor's metrics change while it is laid out, and scrolling then scheduled a frame during the frame.
724. **The peek follows the model by place, not by index.** When the model compares again, the peek shows the change nearest after the line its zone is at, in the same provider, and goes with the last change; while the keyboard is in the small editor the peek stays on its change (the zone moves with its line), so a keystroke in it does not move the peek under the user. VS Code reacts to the model's change event with the same intent (`onDidModelChange`); the rule here is the one the code makes explicit. The title says "changes" when the editor has more than one change (of any provider), and its "N of M" counts the changes of the provider shown, as VS Code does; with a staged change and a work-tree change it reads "1 of 1 changes".
725. **The selection commands read the focused side of a diff pane.** `Documents().Active()` (and with it `git.stageSelectedRanges`, `unstageSelectedRanges`, `revertSelectedRanges`) answers with the editor in front of the group; for a diff pane that is the side that has the keyboard, else the modified side, as VS Code's `activeTextEditor` is. Every editor sets the context key `resourceScheme` in its scope (the `file` or `git` of the key `when` of the staging commands); a provided document of the Git extension has the scheme `git`.
726. **Staging writes the index with `hash-object -w` and `update-index --cacheinfo`.** The text of the index entry is the index version with the changes applied (`applyLineChanges`), the mode of the entry is kept, the file in the work tree is never touched, and a change that is not one of the file's current changes (the file was edited after the peek was made) is refused with a notification (`GitStageStale`) instead of guessing. Revert is one `Documents().Apply` edit (one undo step) of the changes cut out of the document, which is checked to be the text the changes were made for, and the document is saved afterwards, as VS Code's `revertChange` does. The `git apply --cached` of the tests is only an oracle: the commands do not use it.
727. **The toolbar in the gutter of a diff editor is a layer over the modified editor, kept inside it.** VS Code puts it in the gutter at the hovered hunk (`diffEditor/gutter/hunk`) and, when a selection touches a change, at the selection (`diffEditor/gutter/selection`); here it shows for the line the pointer is on, with the selection's toolbar winning when the pointer is on the selected lines. Its buttons are taller (22 px) than a row (19 px): they are centered on the row and clamped inside the viewport while any of the row shows (a first version hid them on the first line of the file, which the app test found). It is not drawn over a read-only side or when no menu item holds.
728. **Menus and keys by VS Code's names, in this window.** `scm/change/title` is the menu `SCMChangeContext`; the Go menu has Next Change and Previous Change (`editor.action.dirtydiff.next`, `previous`, group `7_change_nav`); Alt+F3 and Shift+Alt+F3 work in the small editor of a peek too (the editors of the peek share the controller). The conditions of VS Code that read the `ref` of a `git:` URI read the path of the Git extension's documents (`emetos-virtual:/git/<repository>/<ref>/<path>`, index `""`, comparison `~`): `originalResource =~ /^emetos-virtual:\/git\/[0-9a-f]+\/\/[^/]/` for the index version.
740. **The random branch names (`git.branchRandomName.*`) and the button that makes another name are not built, and the `TODO(step 24.13)` that promised them is changed to say so.** SPEC §10.30 "Not built" lists them (VS Code takes the words from a Node package and the button is an inline button of its input box, which Emetos's `InputBox` has no room for); the TODO that step 24.10 left in the code named them, the SPEC and the step's own text do not. The settings are not registered either (`data_settings.go` says why). If they are wanted: the word lists are a table of a few hundred words in Go, and `InputBoxOptions` needs a `Buttons` list with a callback that sets the text.
741. **`git.autoStash` on makes the checkout that local changes stop stash them, switch, and bring them back, without a question.** The SPEC's words were "`git.autoStash` false does it silently", which contradicts the setting's default (false) and its text ("Stash any changes before pulling and restore them after successful pull"). Read as VS Code's meaning of the setting (stash, do it, restore), the on state is **Migrate Changes** without the question (the untracked files go with the stash). With the setting off the question is asked. The SPEC sentence is corrected.
742. **The question has three answers, as VS Code 1.143's: Stash & Checkout, Migrate Changes, Force Checkout** (the SPEC named two). Migrate Changes is stash, switch, `git stash pop` (the conflict warning of the stash commands applies); Stash & Checkout leaves the changes in the stash. Both ask the stash's questions (unsaved files, the message) as `git.stash -u` does.
743. **Force Checkout runs `git checkout --force <ref>` after the one question; VS Code runs `git.cleanAll` (a second question that also deletes the untracked files) and then checks out.** `--force` throws away the changes of the tracked files that the switch would overwrite and keeps the other untracked files; an untracked file that the target has is overwritten, as git does. Fewer questions and less lost; the message of the question names what happens.
744. **The quick pick of the contract got `Title`, `Separator` (the step's), and also `AlwaysShow` and `OnAccept(typed)`.** The checkout picker needs the text typed in the filter: with "Create new branch..." chosen it is the name of the branch and no box is asked (VS Code's `quickPick.value` into `_branch`). `AlwaysShow` keeps the three command rows when a filter is typed; VS Code rebuilds the list so that they follow the matches, here the filter puts the rows without a match after the ones with one, which looks the same. The title is a line centered above the input (`quickinput.PickOptions.Title`).
745. **A description or detail has no icons in this quick input, so "$(circle-small-filled)" is written as " • "** (VS Code renders the icon as a dot between the parts; the stash picker already used the bullet). The label of a row keeps its icon (`$(git-branch)`, `$(cloud)`, `$(tag)`, `$(lock)` for a protected branch).
746. **A remote branch is deleted after a question; a remote tag is not.** The SPEC asks a confirmation for `git.deleteRemoteBranch` (VS Code asks none, only for a branch that is not fully merged); `git.deleteRemoteTag` has none in the SPEC or in VS Code and keeps none. The tags of a remote are read with `git ls-remote --tags` (it can ask for a password through the askpass of 24.9, and fails with `AuthenticationFailed` or `RemoteConnectionError`).
747. **`git.pushTags` pushes the tags and nothing else** (`git push --tags [<remote of the upstream>]`). VS Code's `_push` goes on after the tags and publishes the branch to a remote it asks for, which reads like a missing `return`; a repository with no remote that can be pushed to says so in a warning (VS Code adds an **Add Remote** button, which comes with `git.addRemote` in step 24.15). The progress and the success notification of pushes are 24.15's.
748. **The Branch submenu has no Merge, Rebase or Publish Branch yet (Merge and Rebase came with 24.14, decision 771)** (VS Code's has them): the commands are step 24.14's and 24.15's, and a menu item that says "not available yet" is worse than none (the Commit submenu of step 24.10 left out Abort Rebase the same way). Their items come with those steps.
749. **`Repo.Checkout` already means the discard of paths (step 24.8), so the switch of the work tree is `Repo.CheckoutRef`**; the other methods have the names of the step (`Branch`, `RenameBranch`, `DeleteBranch`, `DeleteRemoteRef` for `DeleteRemoteBranch` and `DeleteRemoteTag`, `Tag`, `DeleteTag`, `PushTags`, `RefsQuery` for `Refs` with a sort and details, `RemoteTags`, `FindTrackingBranches`). A name that is empty or starts with a dash is refused before git starts (`GitRefInvalid`), so it can not be read as an option.
750. **The picker is shown when the refs are read, not before.** VS Code shows the quick pick busy and fills it; the contract has no busy state, and `git for-each-ref` of a repository of 100,000 refs takes a fraction of a second. The list is read off the UI thread with the sort of `git.branchSortOrder` and the details when `git.showReferenceDetails` is on (author, hash and subject; the date and the arrows in the description).
751. **A checkout given an argument (`{"treeish": "v1.0"}`) has no question about local changes**, as in VS Code (`_checkout` with a treeish calls `repository.checkout` and shows git's own message); only what the user picked has the three answers. The other commands take `{"name": ...}` (a branch to make, rename to or delete; a tag to make or delete) and `{"ref": ...}` for a tag; the Graph's commands (24.20) will pass what they need the same way. `git.pullBeforeCheckout` is read into the job and acts when 24.15 gives it a fast-forward to run first.
752. **The branch item shows the checkout while it runs and is not clickable meanwhile**, as VS Code's (`loading~spin`, the ref being switched to, the tooltip "Checking Out Branch/Tag..."), and the same for a running commit ("Committing Changes...") and sync ("Synchronizing Changes...", set by 24.15). The lock icon for a protected branch is new for Emetos (24.5 left it out). Remote branch protection rules that other extensions contribute do not exist here.
753. **Not built from VS Code's checkout:** the buttons of a remote's items (the remote source actions of GitHub, D33), the handling of a branch that a worktree has checked out (`WorktreeBranchAlreadyUsed`, with step 24.19), the "The new branch will be X" information under the name box (the contract's input box has an error message only; the box shows the error, and the name is made safe when it is accepted), and the selection of the part after the prefix in the name box.
760. **A commit while a merge, a cherry-pick or a revert waits uses the message of the box; with an empty box it runs `git <command> --continue` with no editor, and a rebase always runs `git rebase --continue`.** VS Code runs `git commit` for the first three (and asks for a message when the box is empty) and `rebase --continue` for a rebase, ignoring the box. The SPEC says the commit finishes the operation "with the message of the operation": the box starts with that message (`MERGE_MSG`, read by 24.10), so the typed message wins and the empty box falls back on git's own (`Repo.Continue`, `commit.go`). With `git.useEditorAsCommitInput` on and an empty box the commit goes to the editor tab as any commit does (the setting's meaning is kept).
761. **A cherry-pick and a revert have the Continue button and the placeholder "Message ({0} to continue the merge|rebase|cherry-pick|revert)"; VS Code has them for neither state, and has no continue placeholder at all.** The SPEC (§10.30 "In-progress states") and step 24.10's tasks name the placeholder and the button for the four states; the texts are Emetos's own (the tooltips follow VS Code's "Continue Merge" and "Continue Rebase": "Continue Cherry Pick", "Continue Revert"). The Publish Branch and Sync Changes buttons are off in all four states (VS Code: merge and rebase).
762. **The branch item has the conflicts icon in all four states, also for a rebase.** A rebase has HEAD detached, so VS Code's item shows the commit icon with "(Rebasing)"; the SPEC says the conflicts icon, and a detached HEAD in the middle of a rebase is the case where the user most needs to see that something waits. The lock of a protected branch still comes first. The `!` after the label stays VS Code's (merge and rebase only). The 24.5 test that expected the commit icon for a rebase and the plain branch icon for a cherry-pick follows.
763. **`REVERT_HEAD` is found and shown as a state, but there is no revert command and no Abort Revert** (VS Code has neither; `git revert` is run in the terminal). The commit continues it like a cherry-pick. A revert that is aborted is aborted in the terminal.
764. **`git.rebaseInteractive` ("Rebase Branch (Interactive)...") and `git.rebaseSkip` ("Skip Rebase Commit") are Emetos's own commands.** The step asks for `rebase -i` through the editor helper and for a skip in its tests, and without a command nobody can reach either. Both are in the palette (the Branch submenu has the first, after Rebase Branch); the skip is shown only while a rebase is stopped. The editor helper's environment now also sets `GIT_SEQUENCE_EDITOR` (the list of commits would otherwise go to the user's `sequence.editor`, which may need a terminal); with `git.useEditorAsCommitInput` off, `git.rebaseInteractive` says so in a warning instead of starting a git that cannot ask. The list opens in a tab; saving and closing it goes on, closing it without a save stops the rebase (the answer of 24.9's editor), and a rebase waits in the queue of the repository while the tab is open.
765. **`CherryPickConflict` is the tag of a pick that printed a conflict, not of every failed pick.** VS Code gives it to every failure but the empty pick, so a mistyped hash says "There were merge conflicts"; here the hash gives git's own message ("fatal: bad revision"). `Repo.CherryPick` ends an empty pick with `--abort` (VS Code does) and returns `CherryPickEmpty`.
766. **`git.runGitMerge` merges the stages of the index, VS Code merges the files of the merge editor's inputs.** VS Code passes the `fsPath` of the editor's input URIs to `git merge-file`, which for `git:` URIs are not files; Emetos has no such inputs for a marker file, so it reads stages 2, 1 and 3 of the index (`git cat-file blob :N:path`) into a temporary folder, with the labels HEAD, base and the other side (the branch of `MERGE_MSG`, "abc1234 (subject)" for a rebase, `CHERRY_PICK_HEAD`). It needs two additions to the contract: `Documents().ActiveMergeEditor()` (an extension cannot read `isMergeEditor` or `mergeEditorResultUri`) and the result document is replaced through `Documents().Apply` (one undoable edit; the merge editor follows the change). It refuses a file that lacks a side (deleted by one) with `MergeStageMissing`.
767. **`git.acceptMerge` stages the file on the core event `emetos.mergeEditor.completed`; the command itself takes `{"uri"}` and is not in the palette.** VS Code's command runs `mergeEditor.acceptMerge` and stages the file when it worked; an extension cannot run core commands, and the merge editor's own Complete Merge button and Ctrl+Enter must stage as well, so the window publishes the event after the save (also for the four files of `emetos --merge`, where the extension finds the result outside Merge Changes and does nothing) and the extension stages. VS Code then shows the Source Control view (`workbench.view.scm`); an extension cannot, so the view is not brought to the front. A file completed "with conflicts" is staged too, as in VS Code (the merge editor asked first).
768. **A click on a both-modified or both-added row with `git.mergeEditor` on opens the file in the merge editor through `OpenSpec.MergeEditor`, which starts "Resolve in Merge Editor" of §9.11 (the markers, not the three stages).** VS Code's `git.openMergeEditor` builds the merge from the stages and the commits (`git:` URIs, titles and descriptions of the branches); Emetos's core command (step 9.8) reads the markers of the file, and its panes are labelled by them. With the setting off the file opens in a text editor and the window's notification offers the merge editor (VS Code's `override: false`). The `editor/content` items of VS Code (Resolve in Merge Editor over a file in Merge Changes, Complete Merge over the result) are registered with their conditions, but nothing in the window draws that menu, so the notification and the Complete Merge button of the merge editor stand in for them. `mergeEditorBaseUri` and the `resource` key are not set by the window, so the conditions leave them out or never hold.
769. **A conflict says so in a warning with no "Show Changes" button.** VS Code's button runs `workbench.view.scm`; an extension cannot run it (24.18's stash conflict has the same warning). A merge, a rebase or a cherry-pick that stopped on a conflict is a warning, not an error, and nothing is logged as a failure; the same when `git.commit` continues a rebase and the next commit stops. A merge that local changes would overwrite shows git's own message; VS Code shows "Please clean your repository working tree before checkout." for it, which is the checkout's text.
770. **The box shows the message of the commit a rebase stopped at, and warns when it is changed, as VS Code's `rebaseCommit` does; the extension reads the commit once per stopped commit.** `compute` reads `REBASE_HEAD` (or `stopped-sha`) with `git log -n1`; the box is filled when the commit is not the one the last status saw and emptied when the rebase ends (the template is read again then, `leaveRebase`/`enterRebase`). The message is not sent to git: `git rebase --continue` keeps the message of the commit, which is why the warning exists.
771. **Abort Merge and Abort Cherry Pick are in the Commit submenu with Abort Rebase (`1_commit@5..7`).** VS Code lists only Abort Rebase there and the other two in the palette; the SPEC says every abort is in the palette and the `...` menu. They are always listed, not conditioned on the state (VS Code's Abort Rebase is the same: its enablement is the state, which a menu of Emetos does not read), and the palette shows each only while its state holds.
772. **Contract additions of the step, named for the report:** `extension.OpenSpec.MergeEditor`, `extension.ActiveMerge` and `Documents().ActiveMergeEditor()`, `extension.TopicMergeEditorCompleted` and `MergeEditorCompleted{URI}` (also in `exthost`'s list of core topics), `fakeapi.API.SetActiveMergeEditor`. A host without a workbench answers `ActiveMergeEditor` with false and `OpenDocument` with `ExtensionDocumentUnavailable` as for every other document, and the extension logs nothing for either.
780. **A job's questions are modal dialogs; the question about autofetch is a notification.** The pull asks "It looks like the current branch might have been rebased..." (Always Pull, Pull, Don't Pull), a tag conflict asks to replace the local tags, the sync asks to confirm, a force push and a branch with no upstream ask too. VS Code shows the first as a non-modal message that the pull waits for, which a toast that hides after a few seconds would leave unanswered while a command waits for it; the others are modal in VS Code too. The questions that come in the middle of a job are asked from its goroutine (`remoteWork.ask`, which waits for the answer, for the end of the job's context or for a dialog that cannot be shown) so that a pull is one function. The one question that is not a dialog is autofetch's (decision 788), as in VS Code.
781. **`git.addRemote` asks the URL in an input box, validated; VS Code's picker of remote sources is not built.** VS Code's `pickRemoteSource` is a quick pick of the providers and of the URL typed ("Add remote from URL"); Emetos has no provider (D33), so the URL is an input box ("Provide repository URL", message "Add remote from URL") with `git.ValidateRemoteURL`: a path (absolute, relative, `~`, spaces allowed), a `file`, `http`, `https`, `ssh` or `git` URL with a host (a file URL with an absolute path), or an scp-like `user@host:path`; a dash at the start, control characters, spaces in a URL, other schemes and `host:` alone are refused with a message under the box. 24.16's clone can use the same function. The name box and `sanitizeRemoteName` are VS Code's; the new remote is fetched, as VS Code does, and when that fails the remote stays added (VS Code's too) and the failure is shown.
782. **`git.publish` of a repository with no remote goes straight to Add Remote.** VS Code lists the remote source publishers and says "Your repository has no remotes configured to publish to." when there are none; the SPEC says no service repository is made and `git.addRemote` comes first, so the two boxes of Add Remote open at once and the branch is pushed with `-u` to the remote that was added. `git.push` of a repository with no remote keeps VS Code's warning with **Add Remote** (which adds the remote and stops: VS Code does not push afterwards either).
783. **`git.pushTags` stops after the tags are pushed.** VS Code's `_push` goes on after `pushTags` to the second half, which asks "Pick a remote to publish the branch" as for Push to...; that is a missing `return`. Step 24.13's `git.pushTags` pushed to the remote of the upstream, and so does this one (`git push --tags <remote>`; none given when the branch has no upstream, which is git's choice).
784. **Sync (Rebase) is in the Pull, Push submenu once the repositories are known.** VS Code's `when` is `gitState == idle`, a key nothing sets (the extension sets `git.state`), so its entry is never shown; Emetos uses `git.state == initialized`.
785. **A push of a branch with no upstream asks to publish it with VS Code's question, kept in the profile's state; the types that cannot ask offer a button.** `git.push` and a sync of such a branch ask "The branch "x" has no remote branch. Would you like to publish this branch?" (OK, OK, Don't Ask Again, Cancel); the second answer is kept in `$XDG_STATE_HOME/emetos/git-state.json` (`confirmBranchPublishOff`, mode 0600, written atomically), as VS Code keeps it in its global state, and not in `settings.json`. A push that cannot ask (`git.pushWithTags`) shows git's message with **Publish Branch**, which the SPEC lists. A rejected push offers **Pull** and **Show Git Output** (VS Code only offers its log), and so does a refused force push.
786. **A push the server refuses (`! [remote rejected]`) is the new tag `RemoteRejected` and shows the server's message.** VS Code gives every "failed to push some refs" the code `PushRejected` and the text "Try running Pull first", which is wrong for a protected branch or a hook (the SPEC: "protected-branch refusals as the remote sends them"). `RemoteRejected` is for a push whose refusals are all the server's; the notification is "Git: " and the lines the remote sent (`remote: ...`), else the reason in parentheses. `ForcePushWithLeaseRejected` and `ForcePushWithLeaseIfIncludesRejected` are VS Code's codes, found from the options on the command line and the `(stale info)` or `(remote ref updated since checkout)` of git; `BranchFastForwardRejected` is a fetch of `<ref>:<branch>` that is not a fast-forward.
787. **The first line of git's error output is the message for a remote that cannot be reached.** For `PermissionDenied`, `RemoteConnectionError`, `CantAccessRemote`, `RepositoryNotFound` and `NoRemoteRepositorySpecified` the notification is "Git: " and the first line git printed (`user@host: Permission denied (publickey).`), VS Code's rule for a failure it has no text for; the generic text of the earlier steps, the last line, would be "and the repository exists." for all of them. A fetch or a pull that ssh refuses is a `RemoteConnectionError`, a push a `PermissionDenied` (VS Code's rules, kept).
788. **Autofetch is driven by events, asks one question, and keeps its answer in the state of the profile.** One `autofetcher` for each open repository: on when `git.autofetch` is true or `all`, it fetches silently (`EMETOS_GIT_FETCH_SILENT`: the helper answers a prompt with a cancel) when the repository is idle (no command of Emetos runs: the end of a command and the focus event wake it) and the window focused (tracked from `emetos.window.focusChanged`, true at the start), then waits `git.autofetchPeriod` seconds on the extension's clock (at least one second: a 0 would loop), and turns off after an `AuthenticationFailed`.
