Off, it has no timer and starts no process; switching the setting off and on again starts it again. VS Code's first fetch is also at once. The question "Would you like Emetos to periodically run "git fetch"?" is a notification with **Yes** (sets `git.autofetch` to true in the user settings and remembers), **No** (remembers) and **Ask Me Later**; it is asked after the first fetch, pull, push or sync that worked in a window and only when autofetch is off and not answered (`autofetchInformed` of `git-state.json`); with autofetch on already it counts as answered. A notification that is dismissed with the X counts as Ask Me Later (VS Code's counts as No). A metered connection is not detected.
789. **A second pull, push or sync while one runs is dropped; a fetch is not.** VS Code's `@throttle` runs one more after the first ends; here the same click twice (a double click on the Sync item, the button and a key) is one run, which is what the user meant. The sync item and the buttons are off while one runs anyway.
790. **The sync item follows VS Code's running states, and the remotes are read with every status.** The item spins (`$(sync~spin)`, no click, "Synchronizing Changes...") while a pull, push or sync runs, and says "Committing Changes..." and "Checking Out Changes..." (later states win, as in VS Code). The remotes come from the configuration file with each status, so a remote added in a terminal changes the item (Publish Branch, the arrows) at the next refresh instead of at the next start.
791. **`git.pullBeforeCheckout` fetches `<upstream>:<branch>`, as VS Code does; the TODO of 24.13 named `merge --ff-only`.** `Repo.FastForwardBranch` runs `git fetch <remote> <upstream branch>:<branch>` for a branch with an upstream (the refspec fast-forwards a branch that is not checked out and refuses one that has commits of its own, which is not an error); any failure is written to the Git channel and the checkout goes on.
792. **A commit's post-commit command starts at once and is not awaited; the commits that waited for the commit wait for it.** `git.push` and `git.sync` are real commands now; `runPostCommit` starts them and the sync item spins. What VS Code's wait means here: the commands that came in while the commit ran (`commitQueue`) start once the push or sync ended (`releaseCommits` after `setSyncing(false)`), so a second commit does not run in the middle of a pull.
793. **`git.supportCancellation` shows a notification with Cancel and the percentage of the transfer; only a sync with it asks git for `--progress`.** The notification is "Syncing. Cancelling may cause serious damages to the repository" (`Notifications().Progress`, cancellable); Cancel cancels the context of the fetch and the pull (the process group gets SIGTERM), the push does not start, and the cancellation shows no failure. The percentage is the phase of git's progress mapped to 0 to 100 as VS Code's clone does. The other commands show the progress bar of the repository and the spinning item (`git.showProgress`) and no percentage.
794. **A pull that fails for local changes shows git's message, not VS Code's "Please clean your repository working tree before checkout."**, which is the text for a checkout (24.14's decision 769 for a merge). The tag is `DirtyWorkTree` as before; `git.autoStash` passes `--autostash` (git 2.30 is the oldest git Emetos runs, so VS Code's own stash of git older than 2.27 is not needed). A tag conflict with no remote or no tag names stops with git's error (VS Code throws a plain Error).
795. **Emetos sets no `GIT_HTTP_USER_AGENT`.** VS Code sets one with its name and version on its fetch, pull and push; nothing is sent to a remote beyond what git sends (SPEC §12).
796. **A quick pick separator needs a label.** The host drops an item with an empty label, so the line before "Fetch all remotes" of the remotes list has the label " " (VS Code's separator has none).
797. **The Graph's title actions and the hidden `git._syncAll`.** `git.fetchRef`, `git.pullRef` and `git.pushRef` are implemented (`{"ref": "origin/main"}` names the branch; a ref of no remote does nothing, `git.pushRef` is `git.push`) and are in the palette as VS Code has them, hidden; the Graph that calls them is step 24.20. `git._syncAll` (which syncs every repository) has no caller and is not built.
798. **Contract additions of the step, named for the report:** `extension.Notifications.Progress(ProgressOptions) NotificationProgress` (`Title`, `Cancellable`, `OnCancel`; `Report(message, percent)` and `Done`), `notify.Service.UpdateMessage`, `fakeapi.FakeProgress` and `API.Progresses`. A scope that cannot show notifications gets a handle that does nothing. Error tags added: `GitRemoteInvalid` (a remote or URL that is empty or starts with a dash), `RemoteRejected`, `ForcePushWithLeaseRejected`, `ForcePushWithLeaseIfIncludesRejected` and `BranchFastForwardRejected`.
800. **A clone into a taken name goes to the next free one (`origin-1`), as VS Code's does, and is refused only when 20 names are taken or the caller named the folder.** The step's task says "an existing target refused"; VS Code's `Git.clone` never clones into an existing folder when it picks the name (it counts up to 19), and git itself refuses a folder that has files. `CloneOptions.TargetName` (the `_git.cloneRepository` of VS Code) is used as given: an empty folder is used and a folder with files fails with `GitCloneTargetExists` before git starts, and so do 20 taken names. The folder that was there is never touched.
801. **The question after a clone is "Would you like to open the repository?", the text of the pinned VS Code, not the SPEC's "...the cloned repository?"** (an older VS Code's text); the SPEC sentence of §10.30 was changed. There is no **Add to Workspace** button (no multi-root, §10.14). The question after `git.init` is VS Code's "Would you like to open the initialized repository?" with the same buttons, and **Cancel** there opens the repository in the view without opening the folder, as dismissing VS Code's (non-modal) message does.
802. **A clone runs outside every repository, and a cancelled or failed one removes its folder.** VS Code runs `git clone` in the parent folder (so a parent inside a repository that was not trusted would be refused by the guard of D37 although git reads none of its files) and leaves the partly cloned folder when cancelled; the SPEC says the folder is removed. The runner gets no working directory, a source that is a path is made absolute from the parent folder (`../x.git`) or the home folder of git's environment (`~/x.git`) as VS Code's working directory made it, and only the folder the call made (or the entries of an empty one that was there) is removed. VS Code's `encodeURI` of an address with a space is not done (it would break a path with a space); `ValidateRemoteURL` refuses a space in a URL and allows it in a path.
803. **The welcome content follows VS Code's links.** **Initialize Repository** runs `git.init?%5Btrue%5D` (skipFolderPrompt: the folder of the tab is initialized at once; the plain `git.init` of the first version asked which folder), and the window with no folder has **Clone Repository** = `git.cloneRecursive` (VS Code's; it was `git.clone`). The folder with no repository keeps the second button, **Clone Repository**, that the SPEC and the step ask for and VS Code's `view.workbench.scm.folder` does not have.
804. **The repository cache keys by the address with its user information masked, and cloning an address that is open in the tab says so.** VS Code keys by the address as typed, so `https://user:token@host/x` would be written to the cache file; Emetos writes `https://***@host/x` (two addresses that differ in the user only are one repository). VS Code does nothing when the clone in the cache is the folder of the window; Emetos shows "The repository is open already, in <folder>." so the command does not look broken.
805. **`git.openRepository` picks a folder with the file chooser (VS Code's open dialog).** Step 24.5's version asked the path in an input box; it still takes a path (a string or `{"path"}`) for the tests and the palette's callers. A folder that holds no repository is an error notification ("There is no Git repository in ..."), where VS Code logs only; the repository that holds a folder inside it opens (VS Code's `openIfParent`).
806. **`Workspace().OpenFolder(path, newTab)` is the contract addition that opens a clone; `FileChooserOptions.Directory` chooses a folder.** `newTab` false replaces the folder of the tab (`forceReuseWindow`), true opens a workspace tab or shows the one that has the folder (`forceNewWindow`); it returns before the folder is open (the window asks about unsaved changes first, and the scope of the extension is made again for a replaced folder). Errors: `ExtensionOpenFolderUnavailable`, `ExtensionFilePathInvalid`. Step 24.19's worktree commands use it. `fakeapi.API.OpenedFolders` records the calls. New tags: `GitCloneURLInvalid`, `GitCloneParentInvalid`, `GitCloneParentFailed`, `GitCloneTargetInvalid`, `GitCloneTargetExists`, `ExtensionOpenFolderUnavailable`. `Repo.Clone` of the step's text is `Runner.Clone`, which returns the folder: a clone has no repository yet.
807. **Contract additions of the step, named for the report:** `extension.Workspace.OpenFolder`, `extension.FileChooserOptions.Directory`, `fakeapi.API.OpenedFolders` and `fakeapi.OpenedFolder`, the host's `ScopeOptions.OpenFolder`. A host without a workbench answers `OpenFolder` with `ExtensionOpenFolderUnavailable` and the extension logs nothing for it (and none for `ExtensionFileChooserUnavailable` and `ExtensionDialogsUnavailable`, which `logUnavailable` now leaves out too).
808. **Not built from VS Code's clone and init:** the quick pick of remote source providers (no providers, D33: the address is typed in an input box), **Add to Workspace**, `returnRepositoryPath`, `git.continueInLocalClone`, the `vscode://vscode.git/clone` URI handler (`protocolHandler.ts`), `_git.cloneRepository`, the telemetry of the outcome, `GIT_HTTP_USER_AGENT` (decision 795), and the "learn more" piece of the Explorer's welcome content (it points to documentation).
809. **Finding, not changed: `Workspace.onUI` runs a posted function at once on the posting goroutine while the shell of a workspace is not on screen, and reads `w.shell` and `w.tab` from there.** The real Git extension in a window that was just made (a clone opened in a new tab or replacing the folder, a folder opened by the user) has goroutines (the search for git, the discovery, the first status) that post from the first moment of its activation, so two of them can run their functions at the same time, and with the UI thread, which `-race` reports in a test that starts the real extension in a managed tab (found while testing the clone end to end in the window). Making `onUI` post through the application's binding for a tab that is not shown yet fixes the first half but breaks tests that count on the dialog or the notification arriving at once (`TestGoldenEventSharingPage`) and still reads `w.tab` from the goroutine; `w.shell` and `w.tab` would need to be atomic or the posts queued until the first frame. The tests of step 24.16 use the fake window for the extension and a probe extension for the window (`TestAnExtensionOpensAFolderInItsTabOrInANewOne`) because of it.
820. **Worktrees follow VS Code 1.143 (step 24.19), with the deviations of 821 to 832.** The Git client has `Repo.Worktrees` (`git worktree list --porcelain`, parsed by `ParseWorktrees`; the record of a bare repository is left out), `WorktreeDetails` (adds the subject of the commit), `AddWorktree` (`worktree add [-b branch] [--no-track] -- <path> <commitish>`), `RemoveWorktree` (`worktree remove [--force] -- <path>`), `LsIgnored`, `LsFilesIn` and `CheckIgnoreFolders` (the three commands of the setup) and the tags `WorktreeAlreadyExists`, `WorktreeContainsChanges` and `WorktreeBranchAlreadyUsed` (VS Code's three codes; the last also from a checkout, git 2.42 and later say "used by worktree at", older ones "checked out at"). `worktreeinclude.go` and `worktreesymlink.go` of the client are the ports of VS Code's two files, with their tests. The extension has `git.createWorktree`, `deleteWorktree`, `deleteWorktree2`, `openWorktree`, `openWorktreeInNewWindow`, `migrateWorktreeChanges`, `compareWithWorkspace` and the hidden `git.openWorktreeMergeEditor`, the Worktrees submenu of the `...` menus, the `scm/sourceControl` items of a worktree repository, Compare with Workspace in the menus of the changed files of a worktree, and detection by `git.detectWorktrees` with its limit.
821. **The main work tree is not in the pickers of Delete Worktree and Migrate Worktree Changes.** VS Code's list (`getWorktrees`) starts with the main work tree, which `git worktree remove` refuses and a migration cannot take changes from; `git.migrateWorktreeChanges` there would show a picker for one linked worktree, and returns without a word for none. Emetos lists the linked ones only, takes the only one without asking, and says "This repository has no worktrees." when there is none.
822. **Delete Worktree asks first, in both commands.** VS Code runs `git worktree remove` at once; the SPEC says "a confirmation". The question is "Are you sure you want to delete the worktree "name"?" with the path as its detail and the button **Delete Worktree**, because the folder goes with its ignored files (`node_modules`, `.env`), which git cannot bring back. A worktree with changes asks the second question of VS Code ("The worktree contains modified or untracked files. Do you want to force delete?", detail "Worktree: <path>", **Force Delete**); it does not list the files (the SPEC's "names them" is the path).
823. **The worktree the window shows is never deleted.** VS Code refuses only when the main repository is not open. In Emetos the main repository can be open (it is a repository of the view) while the tab's folder is the worktree, so the refusal ("You cannot delete the worktree you are currently in...") also holds when the folder of the window tab is the worktree or is inside it.
824. **The folder where worktrees go is remembered when it is not the default.** VS Code updates its `worktreeRoot:<root>` state only when one was stored already, so it is never stored. Emetos stores it (`worktreeRoots` in `git-state.json`, by repository root) when the folder of a new worktree is not the one the next worktree would start in, and so the next worktree starts there. The Browse button of the path box leaves the text as it is when the dialog is closed (VS Code empties the box); a worktree path that a worktree has already gets `-1`, `-2`... as in VS Code, and a notification says so (VS Code warns while typing, and the input box of Emetos has only errors, which stop the Enter).
825. **After a worktree was made a notification offers to open it.** The SPEC says it asks "Open the worktree?"; VS Code does not ask. It is a notification (not a modal dialog) with **Open Worktree in Current Window** and **Open Worktree in New Window**. The setup of the symlinks and copies says what it did in an information notification ("Worktree set up: linked 1 of 1 folder(s) and copied 2 of 2 folder(s)/file(s).", the SPEC's "a notification says how many"), and the failures are VS Code's warnings ("Failed to create N worktree folder symlink(s).", "Failed to copy N folder(s)/file(s) to the worktree.").
826. **The folders that the patterns match are found with Emetos's own ignore matcher.** VS Code makes a scratch repository and asks `git check-ignore` there, with the patterns as `core.excludesFile`, to find which folders the patterns alone match. A scratch repository would need the trust of the extension; `parseIgnore` and `matchIgnore` (the matcher the watcher uses, SPEC §10.30 "Refresh") do it with no process, a folder matching when it or a folder above it does. Which folders the repository ignores is still asked of git (`check-ignore --no-index`). The patterns of `git.worktreeIncludeFiles` reach `ls-files --exclude-from` as a file in a private folder of the temporary files, because git reads a pipe as an empty file. Not built: the `worktree-created` tasks (decision 438).
827. **A worktree opened to move changes or to be offered is a repository of the view.** `handleWorktreeConflict` of VS Code opens the repository of the folder (also one that was closed) before the question; so does the migration, which waits for git to open it and for its first status. A folder that is no repository (a path that is taken by something else) is the generic failure with git's message, where VS Code returns without a word.
828. **A conflict of a migration names the sides "Workspace" and "Worktree".** `OpenSpec.MergeLabels` (new contract field) replaces the names the merge editor reads from the markers; `git.openChange` of a conflict of a repository that is migrating, with `git.mergeEditor` on, and `git.openWorktreeMergeEditor` use it. The state ends when the Merge Changes group is empty (VS Code's `isWorktreeMigrating`). The warning of a conflict has no "Show Changes" button (decision 646).
829. **`InputBoxOptions.Buttons` and `OnButton` are contract additions** (VS Code's input box buttons with the inline location): icons after the text, whose function gets the index, the text and a function that replaces the text; the host's quick input draws them as icon buttons in the input and keeps the box open (the path box has `IgnoreFocusOut`) while the folder chooser of the desktop is open. A panic in the function is `ExtensionInputBoxCallbackPanicked`. `fakeapi.Input` has `PressButton`, `Type` and `Value`.
830. **Detection opens a worktree as a repository when `git.detectWorktrees` is on** (VS Code's default is false, and so is Emetos's), at most `git.detectWorktreesLimit` of the list that git gives, the main work tree counting as one (as in VS Code), with the warning once per repository when there are more ("The "app" repository has N worktrees which won't be opened automatically. You can still open each one individually by opening a folder."). The list is read with every status while the setting is on, and not at all when it is off. A worktree is never looked into for more worktrees, and one that is the folder of the window tab is a repository whatever the setting says. The Repositories view shows the `worktree` Codicon for a worktree and `archive` for a submodule (VS Code's icons of the source control; before, every repository had the repository icon), and the title bar name of a worktree (`scmActiveRepositoryName`) is the folder of its main repository.
831. **The palette lists Open Worktree in Current Window and in New Window with more than one repository, as the SPEC says.** VS Code's `package.json` has two palette entries for each of them, `gitOpenRepositoryCount > 1` and `false`; the first one makes the command visible. Compare with Workspace has none (it is in the palette always, as in VS Code, and does nothing without a file).
832. **Handling of a branch that a worktree has checked out (decision 753) is built.** A checkout picked from the picker that git refuses with `WorktreeBranchAlreadyUsed` offers to open that worktree ("Branch "x" is already checked out in the worktree at "<path>"."); from a worktree, a branch that the main work tree has is "Branch "x" is already checked out in the current window." when the main repository is open in the view.
840. **The lines of the Graph are 1 px wide, as VS Code draws them; the SPEC said 2 px.** `scmHistory.ts` writes the SVG paths with `stroke-width: 1px` (the circles are 5 px with a 2 px halo of the row's background). The SPEC's "2 px lines" was a guess; §10.30 now says 1 px.
841. **The commit list is not expandable: a click opens the commit's changes in the multi-file diff editor (`git.viewCommit`), and the tree/list mode of VS Code's newer Graph is not built.** VS Code 1.10x rows can be expanded to the changed files of the commit; the SPEC lists only the click, so a row is a row. The rows have no avatars, no emoji and no links in the message (`scm.graph` has no setting for them), and the hover is made of widgets, not Markdown: the author and the date, the whole message, the statistics (files, insertions in the `scmGraph.historyItemHoverAdditionsForeground` color, deletions) and the references as badges. The hover commands of VS Code's `git/src/hover.ts` (copy hash, open commit) and the chat context are not built.
842. **The incoming and the outgoing rows open a range, through `HistoryProvider.OpenCommand` and the new argument `historyRange` (`extension.HistoryRange{Base, Head, Title}`).** VS Code gives the provider's `viewChanges` command the history item ids; Emetos gives the same command one list of objects (`extension.HistoryArgs` reads it: the source control, the commit, the id of a reference, or the range), so `git.viewCommit` opens a commit or `base..head` (the multi-file editor of 24.17 with the kind `range`). The merge base is the common ancestor of the current branch and the one it tracks.
843. **The merge base of a branch is read from `branch.<name>.vscode-merge-base` and from the reflog ("branch: Created from"), and never written.** VS Code's `getBranchBase` does the same and writes `vscode-merge-base` when it finds the base by the reflog; Emetos leaves the configuration of the user's repository alone (the answer is found again from the reflog each time, which costs one `git reflog` on a branch change). With neither, the base is the default branch of the remote (`refs/remotes/<remote>/HEAD`).
844. **A page that ends before the label of a second parent's reference is laid out with the palette color; a whole-list layout would use the color of the reference.** `core/scm/graph` lays a page out from the lanes the page before it left, which is the point of paging, so a reference that sits on a later page than the merge that starts its lane has no color on the earlier page. `TestSecondParentOnALaterPage` pins it; the other ten cases of `scmHistory.test.ts` give the same rows page by page as whole, at every page size (`TestPagesGiveTheSameRowsAsTheWholeList`), except the one that needs a color from a later page.
845. **The search of the Graph is a case-insensitive fixed-string match on the message, the author and the hash prefix, shown with no lanes, in the order of the whole history.** VS Code's `git log --grep --extended-regexp` takes a regular expression; a text typed in a box is not one. The commits found are listed in `--topo-order` of all the references of the filter, but their parents are dropped (a commit whose parent is not in the list would draw a lane that goes nowhere), so the rows are plain dots. It is done with three `git rev-list` (all, `--author`, `--grep`) and one `git log --no-walk=unsorted` for the page, so a page costs what the walk of the history costs, not more.
846. **The first page of a long history costs a walk of all of it unless the repository has a commit-graph file.** `--topo-order` (as VS Code uses for the Graph) cannot start before git has seen every commit, unless generation numbers are known: 100,000 commits take 0.5 s for any page without the file, 5 to 50 ms with it (`git gc`, `git maintenance` and `fetch.writeCommitGraph` write it). The 300 ms of the SPEC's budget is measured with the file (`TestPerfLongHistory` prints both). Emetos does not write the file itself: it would change the user's `.git` folder for a view. The merge base and the first page are read at the same time, which is what keeps the first read at the cost of one walk.
847. **The reference filter is chosen one reference at a time in a list that comes back with the chosen ones marked.** The quick input has no list with several choices (VS Code's `QuickPick.canSelectMany`): choosing a reference toggles its `$(check)` mark and shows the list again, and "Apply (N selected)" at the top sets the filter; All and Auto set it at once. The filter is kept with the window by repository (`scm.graph` of the saved state of the Source Control view), and is reset to Auto when none of its references is there any more.
848. **The menu of a reference is merged into the menu of its commit, as VS Code does: one submenu for each action, one row for each reference of the commit.** Checkout, Delete Branch and Delete Tag are in the context menu of the commit as submenus ("Checkout >", "Delete Branch >" with the references that the action applies to by `scmHistoryItemRef =~ ...`), made for the moment the menu is shown with `MenuRegistry.AddRemovable`. A badge is not a target of its own.
849. **A silent change of references (an automatic fetch) is told to the Graph by `HistoryRegistration.RefsChanged(HistoryRefsChange{Silent: true})`: it refreshes at the top of the list and shows "Outdated" otherwise.** The Git extension marks a change silent when the status was refreshed by autofetch and no command of its own was running (`repository.af.running`). `git.currentHistoryItemIsAhead` and `...IsBehind` of VS Code are not set: nothing in `package.json` reads them.
850. **`git.graph.checkout` of a remote branch checks out the local branch that tracks it, or makes one (`git checkout --track`); `git.graph.deleteBranch` of a remote branch asks first and refuses the branch the current one tracks.** VS Code's `_checkout` does the same for the first (it checks out the tracking branch's name); for the second it deletes the branch with the remote's name without a question. The question is the one `git.deleteRemoteBranch` asks (step 24.13). The current local branch and its remote branch are refused with a notification ("The active branch cannot be deleted.").
851. **A branch or a tag made from a commit of the Graph starts at that commit (`git.branch` and `git.createTag` read the history item id), and `git.fetchRef`, `git.pullRef` and `git.pushRef` with no `ref` argument take the branch the current one tracks.** These are the title actions of the Graph, which are run with the source control alone as the argument.
852. **The Git client's `Log` takes `Revs`, `Decorate`, `TopoOrder`, `Fixed`, `RootsOnly` and `NoWalk`, and a revision that starts with `-` is refused (`GitRevisionInvalid`) before git runs; `Repo.RevList`, `Reflog`, `SymbolicRef` and `ResolveCommit` are new.** The history is walked from the revisions of the references the view asks for (hashes, not names, so that a branch cannot be mistaken for an option), after `--end-of-options`. The status snapshot of a repository now also reads `refs/remotes`, which the base reference of the Graph needs; nothing else of the snapshot reads remote references.
853. **The Graph view is in the list while `scm.historyProviderCount != 0` and a host with no workbench answers `ExtensionHistoryUnavailable`, which the Git extension does not log.** The contract is `SourceControl.SetHistoryProvider` (`extension/history.go`, with the conformance suite `extension/historytest`); a provider that panics fails the extension (`ExtensionHistoryPanicked`), as the other providers do.

## Step 24.23: terminal authentication, cross-checks and the desktop pass

860. **`TerminalEnvironment().Contribute` takes a source function, not a map (deviation from SPEC §10.30 and decision 435).** `Contribute(func() (variables map[string]string, description string)) (dispose, error)`; the Terminal extension asks every source with `Contributions()` when it starts a shell, on the UI thread. Reason: Git's variables point at the socket of its helper server, which Emetos starts only when something needs it (step 24.9, decision 621 and the SPEC's "As built in step 24.9" (3): the first command that can ask starts the server, and an extension that never runs one starts none); a map given at activation would start a server, three scripts and a socket in the runtime folder of every window tab that has Git on, whether or not a terminal is ever opened. With the function the server starts with the first terminal, and the settings (`git.terminalAuthentication`, `git.terminalGitEditor`, `git.useIntegratedAskPass`, `git.useEditorAsCommitInput`, `git.enabled`) are read at that moment, so there is no refresh on a change of a setting as VS Code's `TerminalEnvironmentManager` has: the next terminal gets the new answer. Only VS Code's "replace" mutator exists (no append or prepend: Git uses replace only); a later contribution wins over an earlier one and over the settings of the terminal. No privilege is needed: the extensions are compiled in and trusted (D18). Panics in a source fail its extension (`ExtensionTerminalEnvironmentPanicked`); a variable that cannot be in an environment (no name, `=` or NUL in the name, NUL in the value) is logged and left out.
861. **A terminal gets what VS Code's `getTerminalEnv` gives: `GIT_ASKPASS` (with `git.useIntegratedAskPass` and `git.terminalAuthentication`) and `GIT_EDITOR` (with `git.useEditorAsCommitInput` and `git.terminalGitEditor`), and the socket and the token (`EMETOS_GIT_SOCKET`, `EMETOS_GIT_TOKEN`); no `SSH_ASKPASS`, no `SSH_ASKPASS_REQUIRE`, no `GIT_SEQUENCE_EDITOR`, no `GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0`.** (`Server.TerminalEnvironment`.) ssh in a terminal asks in the terminal, where the person types; a todo list of `git rebase -i` keeps the person's own editor unless `git.terminalGitEditor` is on (git uses `GIT_EDITOR` for it then). The token is in the environment of every program started in that terminal, as VS Code's IPC handle is; the socket answers only the user (`SO_PEERCRED`) and only with the token, and both live as long as the process. The description of the contribution is VS Code's ("Enables the following features: git auth provider, git editor"); the tab's hover of a terminal that got variables adds VS Code's "The following extensions have contributed to this terminal's environment:" with a line for each extension (its name and the description), never the values. A terminal that was running keeps its environment; VS Code's "relaunch needed" state and its Relaunch action are not built (the Terminal has no Relaunch command): "Terminals need to be restarted to pick up a change in this setting", as the settings say.
862. **Escape in the box of a password asked by git typed in a terminal does not fail the command: git asks in the terminal itself.** The helper answers a cancel with exit status 1, and git falls back to its terminal prompt when `GIT_ASKPASS` fails and `GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT` is not 0 (the terminal does not set it, as VS Code's does not). The person can type the password there or press Ctrl+C. This is VS Code's behavior and git's; the test `TestEscapeInTheBoxDoesNotPushAndGitAsksInTheTerminalItself` pins it. `TerminalShellExecutionManager` of VS Code's `terminal.ts` (a status refresh after `git commit` and the like finished in a terminal, through shell integration events) is not built: the watcher of the repository sees the changes the same way (SPEC §10.30: no polling, the watcher and the focus event drive the refresh).
863. **`scm.openInIntegratedTerminal` and `scm.openInTerminal`** (VS Code's ids, in the group `99_terminal` of the context menu of a repository). The first runs the Terminal's `workbench.action.terminal.new` with `{cwd: <root of the repository>}` (it focuses the new terminal and shows the panel); with no source control in the arguments it does nothing, with several it uses the first (VS Code uses the focused row of the Repositories list, which the arguments do not carry), and with the Terminal turned off it says so in a notification. The second starts the program of `terminal.external.linuxExec` (new, application scope) in the folder, in its own session, with a goroutine that collects it (`internal/core/externalterminal`, a port of the Linux part of VS Code's `externalTerminalService.ts`); an empty setting chooses as VS Code does (x-terminal-emulator on Debian, gnome-terminal, konsole, `$COLORTERM`, `$TERM`, xterm) instead of showing the computed default in the setting, which VS Code does and a setting default of Emetos cannot. The rows follow `terminal.sourceControlRepositoriesKind` (`integrated` by default, `external`, `both`); VS Code also asks for a local window (`RemoteNameContext == ''`), which Emetos has no key for (the host's own windows are always local; a client of Remote Work is refused, below). `terminal.explorerKind` and the Explorer's own rows are not registered: the Explorer has no such rows. Both commands are refused to clients of a shared workspace with the class `terminal` (`command.ClientRefusal`): one makes a terminal on the host, the other a window on the host's desktop. They are commands of the workbench, not of Git, so D38 is not touched.
864. **(Resolved by step 24.25, decisions 870 to 880.)** **D38 at the level of policy, with the gap in the headless view stated (to decide).** What is built and tested: no command of the Git extension, and none of the workbench's own source control commands but the two above, is refused to a client (`TestNoGitCommandIsRefusedToAClient`, and the refusal list names no `git.`, `scm.` or `workbench.scm.` prefix); `fetch`, `pull`, `push`, `sync` and `clone` each ask the credential prompt of the host's window and use the host's git, helper and credentials (`TestFetchPullPushSyncAndCloneAskTheHostForItsCredentials`, one command after the other against `HTTPBackend`); what the Git extension puts in an editor (the quick diff marks, the blame text) is in the editor of a headless view, since its services belong to the window tab (`internal/gui/app/headless_scm_test.go`). **What is not there, and the task text of step 24.23 assumes it is:** the Source Control view, the Graph and the Timeline are parts of the shell of a window (`initSCM`, `initTimeline`), the shell of a headless view (`initView`) has none of them, and the view's workspace has no contributions of extensions at all (SPEC §10.18 "Not in the view": their commands, menu items, views and status items live in the scope of the window tab, and the prompts of an extension go to the quick input of the window, not of the view). So a client that looks at the picture of a view cannot run `git.fetch` or see a credential box today, whatever the refusal list says. The test of the step ("drives fetch, push, sync and clone through the remote view, the prompt appearing in the view") cannot be written without work on the host that the step does not list: forwarding the extensions' commands and menus into a view's registry, routing the prompts of an extension to the view that started the command (a prompt arrives later, from a goroutine, so the command has to be remembered), and the Source Control, Graph and Timeline panes in `initView`. I did not build that; it is a step of its own (a "contributions in the headless view" step before M25 or in M28). Until then D38 holds for every path a client has now (the core request topics, where Git is allowed) and for the future ones, by the test that the list names no Git command.
865. **The performance numbers of this step are from another machine than the earlier sections of `docs/PERFORMANCE.md`** (a Ryzen 9 6900HX with 16 threads, 61 GB, against the Ryzen AI Max+ 395 of the earlier ones); the section says so. Every Git target of §14 is met except the first page of a 100,000-commit history **without a commit-graph file** (476 ms against 300 ms: git's own walk, decision 853; 5 ms with the file). The idle row is the test `TestIdleDoesNothing`; the CPU of the real window at idle with the GPU is the user's (`scripts/perf.sh --session`).
866. **`placeholder` of the Git extension's commands is gone.** Every command of the manifest has a handler; the `TODO(step 24.9 to 24.23)` is resolved by `TestEveryCommandOfTheManifestHasAHandler`, and a command of the manifest without one is logged at error level and left unbound (it cannot happen in a build that passes the test).

## Step 24.25: extensions and the terminal in the Remote Work view

870. **A view has the extensions' contributions by inheriting the parent's registries, read live, not by copying them.** `command.Registry.Inherit(parent, origin)`, `MenuRegistry.Inherit`, `ViewRegistry.Inherit` and `StatusBar.Inherit` make the registries of the headless view answer for what the window's scope registered (commands the registry lacks, menu items marked `MenuItem.Extension`, containers and views the view lacks, status items whose id starts with `extension.`). Nothing is replayed when a view is made or when an extension comes or goes, and no second extension is activated. The price is that "what is an extension's" is decided by absence (a command the process's registry does not have) and by marks, so a built-in command the view's shell does not bind has no handler in the view (as before).
871. **The origin is a number carried in `command.Context`, and `api.Origins()` is the one new thing an extension sees** (`Current`, `Run`, `Remember`, `Recall`; `Origin` fields on `QuickPickOptions`, `InputBoxOptions` and `DialogSpec`; `ContextKeys().SetFor`). `Current()` is exact on the UI thread inside a handler and in the callbacks that continue it (the host re-enters the origin for the end of a quick pick, input box, dialog, notification action); work that goes on after the handler (a goroutine whose end is handed to `Post`) must keep the origin itself and call `Origins().Run`. The Git extension does it in `mutate` and the clone; a path that does not (an extension that forgets) shows its later questions on the host's window, which is the behavior before this step. Alternatives rejected: a `ctx` on `Show` (a signature change of every call, and the Git extension's callbacks have none) and guessing the screen that "was last active" (wrong with two clients).
872. **The origin reaches git over the helper's socket.** `git.Config.ExtraFor` takes the context (a value that carries the origin), the helper program sends `EMETOS_ORIGIN` and `EMETOS_TERMINAL` (new fields of the request), and the prompt queue is one for each screen. A password that git asks for in a terminal goes to the screen that typed last in that terminal (the Terminal extension remembers it, the shell has `EMETOS_TERMINAL`), so the proof shows the box in the view that typed `git push`. Two clients at once each get a box at once only for different repositories: the commands of one repository run one after the other (its queue), so the second box waits for the first command.
873. **A question whose view is gone is cancelled** (`Hidden` or a cancelled dialog), not shown on the host's window: nobody can answer it there on the client's behalf.
874. **Notifications carry the origin too** (`Notification.Origin`, `Notification.ShownOn`): a toast is shown on the screen that asked and on no other; a notification no command asked for is on every screen as before; the notification center (the service is shared) lists all of them. `Presenter.SetScreen` and the view's `OnNotification` follow the rule.
875. **Context keys are per screen where they describe a person's focus.** `ContextKeys().Set` is for every screen (the host's keys, copied to every view: `Scope.SharedKeys`, `OnSharedKey`); `SetFor(origin, ...)` is for one. The Terminal extension sets `terminalFocus` and the other keys of the keyboard with `SetFor` and the keys that count terminals with `Set`. The focus of a view of an extension (`focusedView`) is noted in the keys of the screen the box is on. The fake API's `SetFor(HostOrigin)` also sets what `ContextKey` reads, so the earlier tests of the Terminal extension are unchanged.
876. **The terminal has one pane for each screen over one emulator.** `entry.views` by origin; the size of the terminal is the size of the pane of the screen that typed last (`Terminal.TypedFrom`, `ResizeFrom`); a pane of another screen draws the terminal clipped or with empty space. The emulator's `Snapshot` keeps what changed for one reader, so a second reader uses `Emulator.SnapshotCopy` (the whole screen, no bookkeeping touched) when the terminal's revision moved. The tabs list and the active terminal are shared: a client that selects a tab selects it on the host too (the SPEC says "as a shared tmux session"; tmux's per-client current window was not built). A client's view that goes off screen drops its panes (the scroll and the selection of that pane are lost).
877. **`workbench.action.createTerminalEditor`, "Move into Editor Area" and the terminals of editor tabs open on the host's window**, since a view has no extension editor tabs; the class `terminal` is removed whole, as asked. `scm.openInTerminal` (the desktop's terminal program, which opens a window on the host's screen) is refused with the class `window` instead.
878. **A clone started in a view asks for its folder in an input box of that view**, not in the file chooser of the desktop (which would open on the host's screen); the path is typed.
879. **`Workspace.Extensions()` of a view is its parent's scope**, so the bodies, title actions, badges and welcome content of the extensions' views are built for the view. The extensions' editor kinds, file views and tabs are not drawn in a view that has none open; a file view of an extension for a file opened in a view is built in the view (not tested).
880. **A dev view for the proof.** `dev.view.show`, `dev.view.run` and `dev.view.type` (debug builds, SPEC §15.2) make a headless view of the workspace, draw its picture over the window, run a command in the view's registry and type into the view with key events, so that one `scripts/proof.sh` run shows the view of a client (step 24.25 asked for "a dev command that opens a headless view of the fixture in a window tab"; the picture is drawn over the window, not in a tab, to keep it in one file; the picture is the view's own, scaled to the window).

## Step 24b.1: what every pack shares

900. **The host got a `ServerLauncher` (`LanguageServers().SetLauncher`, `LaunchRequest`, `ServerLaunch`) so that a pack can find its server.** The step asked for the finder with "`PATH` and the extra places", versions, roots and options built from settings, but the host started a fixed `Program` from `PATH` with static `InitializationOptions` and could not know `~/.cargo/bin`, a folder's `node_modules/.bin`, a version or a project root. The alternatives were a growing list of static fields in `LanguageServerSpec` (extra folders, markers, a version command, option templates) that still could not read the Python interpreter or the jdtls command line, or the extension declaring servers at `Manifest()` time like the Docker extension does (no settings are readable then). The launcher is one function called at every start on a goroutine of the host (so `--version` never blocks the UI), with a thread-safe settings reader; it answers with the program, arguments, environment, root, options and `workspace/configuration` answers. A launcher that fails leaves the server `ServerMissing` (no new state); the host's `Program`/`PathSetting` path is unchanged for the packs that do not set one (Go, Docker). Both implementers (the host and `fakeapi`) have it; the fake keeps the launcher and runs it on demand.
901. **The path settings of the packs are `machine`-scoped settings the pack declares, not the host's `PathSetting`.** A workspace's `.emetos/settings.json` can set a `window`-scoped path (Go's `go.goplsPath` is one), which makes opening a folder able to choose the program Emetos runs. The packs' path settings (`rust-analyzer.server.path`, ...) are in the user's settings only. Go's is left as it is (SPEC says its code is not touched); whether to make it `machine` too is for the user to decide. The consequence: the host does not restart a missing server when the path changes (that is `PathSetting`'s job); the controller restarts it itself.
902. **A restart on a change of the settings is decided by the keys the start read.** `Settings` (the reader the finder and `Customize` get) remembers every key it is asked for, with the fingerprint of the value; the controller compares them on each settings change and calls `Restart` when one differs. Nothing to list by hand per pack, and a pack cannot forget one; the settings the server asks through `workspace/configuration` stay the host's `ConfigurationSection` and are sent with `didChangeConfiguration` without a restart.
903. **One server per tab, started in the nearest project root of the folder, not one server per root.** SPEC §10.34 says "per workspace tab and per root". The host's controller is one per tab and server and the extension cannot add controllers, so `ResolveRoot` picks the root for the folder opened (the folder, or the nearest folder above with a marker, never crossing a `.git`, the home folder or `/`, at most twelve levels) and `FindProjects` lists roots below for the pickers of C#, F# and Java. A tab opened on a monorepo with several roots gets one server on the folder, which is what VS Code's clients do for a single-folder window. Several servers in one tab would be a change of the host (a server instance per root) and is not in M24b's steps.
904. **"How to Install" shows a dialog with the text, not the README's section.** An extension cannot open an address, and the README's Languages section is written in 24b.10. The dialog has what the server gives, the command for this system, the other commands, the server's own address and the path setting, and a Copy Install Command button. When 24b.10 has the README section and an "open address" capability exists, the address can be added to the same text.
905. **`LanguageServerSpec.TraceKey`.** SPEC says the trace setting is `<pack>.trace.server` in VS Code's names (`rust-analyzer.trace.server`); the host's name is `<extension id>.<server id>.trace.server` (`emetos.golang.gopls.trace.server` for Go). A pack may now name the key; it is registered, read and watched exactly as the qualified one, which stays the default (Go keeps it).
906. **Too old refuses the start, an unreadable version does not.** `VersionCheck.Min` fails the start with `ServerTooOld` and a notification with the install help; a program that prints no version, or fails on `--version`, runs anyway, with the reason in `Launch.VersionErr` (written at debug level). The first rule is for jdtls's Java 21 and a TypeScript that is too old; the second keeps a server that has no way to tell its version usable. A pack that needs a version only for a feature reads `Controller.Version()`.
907. **The install command is chosen by the family in `/etc/os-release` and is only copied.** Fedora (and the Red Hat family), Debian (and Ubuntu, Mint), Arch and openSUSE are told apart; any other system, or a family with no command of its own, gets the command marked for every system (`rustup`, `npm`, `dotnet tool`), else the first. The file is read when the menu or the notification needs it. Emetos runs nothing (D47); a test asserts the clipboard and that no program ran.
908. **The missing-server notification is a warning, once per tab for each distinct message.** Go's is a warning too; the host's own "not found" for a server without `OwnUI` is an error. Telling again when the problem changes (not found, then too old after an install of an old one, then a wrong path) matches what a user fixing it sees, and a repeated identical failure (several documents, several restarts) says nothing more.
909. **Set Path... writes with `Settings().Update` and does nothing for a client of a hosted workspace.** The setting is the host's (§10.18, the class `settings`); the client gets an information saying so. A command-less notification button has no `ClientRefusal` to rely on, so the check is the origin (`Origins().Current() != HostOrigin`).

## Step 24b.2: rust-analyzer

920. **The host got the protocol of a server's own: `LanguageServers().Request`, `OnNotification`, `PositionEncoding`, and `ServerLaunch.ClientExperimental`.** The commands the spec lists (Expand Macro, the syntax tree, parent module, join lines, matching brace, reload workspace, Open Cargo.toml) are requests of rust-analyzer's own methods, and `experimental/serverStatus` is a notification; `ExecuteCommand` and `ApplyCodeAction` reach neither, and the server only sends the status when the client says so in its capabilities. The alternatives were one method per request (growing the API for each pack of M24b: clangd's switch header/source, jdtls's commands) or the extension talking to the server itself (it does not own the connection). `Request` **blocks** and is called off the UI thread: the two providers that need it (the code lenses and the text of a read-only document) already run there, and a command hands the call to a goroutine and comes back with `Post`; an asynchronous `done` callback would have forced every provider to wait on a channel anyway. The params are the server's own JSON, so the positions in them are in the encoding the server chose; `PositionEncoding` tells which and `langserver.ToProtocol`/`FromProtocol` convert (Emetos offers UTF-8, and rust-analyzer takes it). Both implementers (host and `fakeapi`) have all of it. `ClientExperimental` replaces nothing of the capabilities Emetos advertises; it fills the `experimental` member.
921. **The Run lenses are the pack's own, built from `experimental/runnables`, and they run `emetos.tasks.run` with the command line.** The spec says the server's Run lenses run through the Tasks extension. rust-analyzer's lenses name `rust-analyzer.runSingle`; the window runs a lens command that is not the server's as a window command with its **first argument only**, and an extension has no way to run another extension's command (it cannot hold `commands.execute`, which only the Terminal has, and an event request to Tasks would fail with `EventNoHandler` until the panel was opened once; making Tasks start with the window would change every window). A lens of the pack's own provider can name any command, so the pack asks for the runnables and gives lenses whose command is `emetos.tasks.run` with the command line (no change to Tasks). The price: `rust-analyzer.lens.enable` and `lens.run.enable` are honoured by the pack, and the lenses that need other client commands (`showReferences` for implementations and references, `debugSingle`) are off, because the capability `experimental.commands.commands` is sent empty so that the server does not send lenses naming commands Emetos lacks. M26 brings the debug lens back with the debugger.
922. **All of rust-analyzer's server settings are registered (197, with VS Code's names), the ones that differ from their default are sent, and any change restarts the server.** The settings editor shows them in the category "Rust Analyzer". Sending only non-defaults keeps the defaults of the server's version in force (the defaults in `package.json` change from release to release). Step 24b.1's rule is that every setting read at a start restarts the server when it changes; rust-analyzer could take `workspace/didChangeConfiguration` for most of them, and VS Code's extension restarts only for a few, so a change of `rust-analyzer.check.command` costs a reload that VS Code spares. Doing it right needs the launcher's `Configuration` answers to be live and a way to send the notification; left for the user to decide.
923. **What only the VS Code client of rust-analyzer uses is not registered** (`debug.*`, `statusBar.*`, `trace.extension`, `server.extraEnv`, `showSyntaxTree`, `testExplorer`, `initializeStopped`, `restartServerOnConfigChange`, `showUnlinkedFileNotification`, `showRequestFailedErrorNotification`, `showDependenciesExplorer`, the problem matcher and ask-before-update of runnables, `semanticHighlighting.nonStandardTokens`, `typing.continueCommentsOnNewline`). `server.extraEnv` is in no spec line; `rust-analyzer.trace.server` is the host's through `TraceKey`.
924. **No minimum version, and a rustup proxy is refused.** Releases are `0.3.N` and rustup's are `1.NN.0`, and no feature of the pack needs a version, so `VersionCheck.Min` is empty. `~/.cargo/bin/rust-analyzer` is a rustup proxy that, without the component, prints an error and exits 1; the finder would take it and the host would restart it five times. A rust-analyzer that prints no version is therefore not started (`RustAnalyzerVersionFailed`) and the message says to run `rustup component add rust-analyzer`; the cost is that a wrapper script without `--version` needs one.
925. **The status item takes `Health` (spinner while the server is not quiescent, warning and error with the message).** `langserver.Controller.SetHealth` is general; the other packs may use it. Progress from `$/progress` still wins, and a restart forgets the health.
926. **`scripts/tool-versions.txt` and `scripts/fetch-tools.sh` start here.** The pinned release binary (version 2026-10-26, `rust-analyzer 0.3.3097-standalone`, sha256 of the download and of the program) and rust-analyzer's `package.json` (sha256) are fetched into `/var/tmp/emetos-tools`. The package.json is pinned by hash in this file, not as a git source in `scripts/reference-versions.txt` (that file is changed only on the user's request); `scripts/update-rust-analyzer-settings.sh` writes `extensions/rust/ra-settings.json` from it. The later steps of M24b add their lines.
927. **The real-server test is in `internal/gui/exthost`, not in `extensions/rust`.** The rig that runs a real host with a tab, documents and the features registry is unexported test code of that package (the Go tests of 14.1 are there too); an extension package has only the fake API. The test imports `extensions/rust` as the test files of `internal/gui` may. The machine has no `rust-src`, so rust-analyzer says "can't load standard library" and cannot resolve `std`; the crate under `testdata/rust/crate` uses no item of it (no `println!`, no `Vec`), and the tests that need `std` would need `rust-src` in `/var/tmp/emetos-tools`.
928. **The pack is named "Rust" (it was "Rust Language Basics").** It is more than the basics now; the Go pack is "Go".

## Step 24b.3: clangd

940. **A manifest can name settings of the editor that its extension may write (`Contributes.WritesSettings`).** vscode-clangd's `clangd.inlayHints.toggle` flips `editor.inlayHints.enabled`, and decision 173 limits `Settings().Update` to the settings an extension declares or registered. Nothing else in the API reaches a setting of the editor (a command cannot be run by an extension, and `commands.execute` is the Terminal's only). The exception is narrow: a key is written only if the extension lists it, so it shows in the manifest; every other key is still `ExtensionSettingNotOwned`; `CheckManifest` refuses an empty key, a duplicate and the extension's own keys (`ExtensionSettingWriteInvalid`). The alternatives were a core command "toggle inlay hints" (an extension cannot run it) or a quick pick that opens the Settings editor at the setting (not a toggle). The user may prefer either; the pack's only use is that one key. A client of a hosted workspace is told it is a setting of the host (as Set Path... is), and nothing is written.
941. **`clangd.arguments`, `clangd.trace` and `clangd.path` are user settings only; `clangd.fallbackFlags` may be set by a folder.** `--query-driver` whitelists programs clangd may run to learn the system headers, and `--log`, `--pch-storage` and `CLANGD_TRACE` name files and folders it writes, so a folder's `.emetos/settings.json` must not decide them (the rule of decision 901 for the path). VS Code lets a workspace set them; flags for a file (`-std=c++20`, `-I include`) are what a project's own compilation database would give anyway.
942. **Only four of vscode-clangd's 17 settings are registered** (`path`, `arguments`, `fallbackFlags`, `trace`), with `clangd.trace.server` as the host's traffic trace. The rest are client behavior of VS Code (`enableCodeCompletion`, `enableHover`, `serverCompletionRanking` through middleware, `semanticHighlighting` (deprecated there), `inactiveRegions.*` (clangd only sends them to a client that says it understands them), `restartAfterCrash`, `checkUpdates`, `detectExtensionConflicts`, `useScriptAsExecutable`), or things Emetos does itself (`onConfigChanged`: clangd re-reads `.clangd` on its own, and a change of `clangd.arguments` restarts the server). `clangd.enable` is not there either: a pack is turned off on the Extensions page. The descriptions are written for Emetos, so `NOTICE` has no new item; the ids and names of vscode-clangd (Apache-2.0 with the LLVM exception) are only names.
943. **The missing compilation database is found by looking, when something changes, not by a watcher.** The item is drawn again when the editor in front changes, the server's state changes, clangd tells a file's state and a setting changes: a handful of `stat` calls, and the file clangd status arrives often enough while a file is worked on that the item notices a `compile_commands.json` written by a build within a moment. A watcher over the folder (recursive for `build/`) costs more than that for the one line it would save. "None" means none of `compile_commands.json` (root, `build/`, the folder of `--compile-commands-dir`), `compile_flags.txt` and a `.clangd` with `CompileFlags:`, and no `clangd.fallbackFlags`. Cost: a project whose database is in another folder that only clangd's own search finds (the parent of the root, the usual CMake out-of-source `../build-x`) is told it has none; it says so only in the tooltip and the label, and `--compile-commands-dir` or `CompilationDatabase:` in `.clangd` silences it.
944. **The offer to create `.clangd` is the command `clangd.projectConfig` and a menu entry, not a notification, and the file is written at once.** A notification for every C file of a project without a database is noise for a single-file C user; the status item says it, the command and the menu offer it. `clangd.projectConfig` is vscode-clangd's command (it opens the file); it also creates, after a question, so that the offer needs no command of its own. The file is made with `O_EXCL` and the stub (`CompileFlags: Add: [-Wall]` and comments) rather than as an unsaved editor, so that clangd sees it as soon as the user looks at it and an existing file cannot be overwritten. It goes in the root clangd works in. `clangd.userConfig` (the file in the user's configuration folder) is not built: it is outside the project.
945. **The AST is asked for the selection (the cursor when it is empty), as vscode-clangd asks, and shown as indented text in a read-only tab.** vscode-clangd shows a tree view in the side bar, which Emetos's extensions cannot add to a language pack without a view of its own; the spec says a read-only tab. A whole-file tree would need a range that clangd answers for the translation unit, and the node at the cursor is what the user wants to see. The tab is one document, replaced by the next answer.
946. **No minimum version of clangd.** `textDocument/ast` (clangd 10) and `switchSourceHeader` are older than any clangd that is installed today and the features Emetos uses beyond them (inlay hints, clangd 14) simply do not appear in an older server. The system's clangd is 22.1.8 (`scripts/tool-versions.txt` records it), not fetched or pinned: D47 says Emetos installs nothing, and the user's task for this step says the system's is used.
947. **The pack is named "C and C++" (it was "C and C++ Language Basics"); the category of its commands is "clangd"** (vscode-clangd's). The Go and Rust packs went the same way (decision 928).
948. **The fixture is a CMake project whose `compile_commands.json` is a template** (`testdata/cpp/project/compile_commands.json.in`): CMake writes absolute paths, and clangd resolves a relative `directory` against the folder of each file, not the database. The test fills in `@DIR@` for its temporary copy. The proof image is made from a project configured by the system's `cmake` into `/tmp/emetos-proof-24b.3`, so that its database is the one CMake writes; clangd's background index writes `.cache/clangd` into a project, which the proof's explorer shows.
949. **The read-only tab code of the Rust pack is copied, not shared.** `extensions/cpp/content.go` repeats the provider, the untitled fallback and the "no warning in a scope that cannot show documents" rule of `extensions/rust/content.go` (about 80 lines). A shared helper in `extensions/internal/langserver` would be the next pack's (jdtls's class files, csharp-ls's metadata are the same shape); step 24b.10 or the first of those packs can extract it with its own tests, and Rust's behavior stays as 24b.2 left it.

## Step 24b.4: Python

960. **Extensions got a place to keep a value: `api.State()` (`Get`, `Set`, `extension.StateValue`).** The step asks for the interpreter chosen "per folder, kept in Emetos's state", and an extension had nowhere to keep anything: `Settings().Update` writes the user's `settings.json` (a path per folder there would be noise the user did not write), and view state is saved only for views that have a body. The new API is small: a JSON value (64 KiB at most) under a key, for the extension and the folder of the scope, in the profile's `state.json` (`GlobalState.Extensions`, extension id, folder, key), read and written from any goroutine, because the language server's launcher reads the choice off the UI thread. A scope that keeps no state (`ScopeOptions.StateGet/StateSet` unset) answers `ExtensionStateUnavailable` and reads nothing. New tags: `ExtensionStateInvalid`, `ExtensionStateUnavailable` (route tests in `exthost/state_test.go`, `storage/extensionstate_test.go`). A client of a hosted workspace has no API of its own: the choice is the host's (decision 964).
961. **A folder's own `.venv`, `venv` or `env` is offered, never taken: Emetos has no workspace trust.** Pyright, basedpyright and pylsp run the interpreter they are given (they ask it for its search paths), and the server is also looked for in the interpreter's `bin` folder, so choosing the folder's own environment on open would run a program that the folder brought (D37 made the same choice for `git`). The picker lists the environments of the folder first, and the first time a Python file is in front in a tab with nothing chosen, an information notification says "This folder has a Python environment, .venv. Use it ...? Emetos then runs the Python programs inside it." with **Use .venv** and **Select Another...**; the answer is kept like any choice. Until then the rules are: the choice, `python.defaultInterpreterPath`, `VIRTUAL_ENV`, `CONDA_PREFIX`, the first `python3` (then `python`) on `PATH`. VS Code asks the same question through its trust prompt. The cost is one click for the common case; the notification is not shown when that environment is the one already in use (the user started Emetos from it). For the same reason **Emetos never runs a program inside the folder to describe it**: the version of such an environment comes from its `pyvenv.cfg` (and is empty when it has none); programs outside the folder are run with `--version`, in their own process group with the 15 s limit of 24b.1.
962. **The three servers are one server in the manifest.** `python.languageServer` chooses which program serves the id `python` (one status item, one Output channel "Python", one trace key `python.trace.server`), with each program's own path setting, install help and version check (`Config.Programs`, `Config.Select` of 24b.1). So that the item and the messages name the program that runs ("basedpyright 1.40.2: <path>", "pyright not found"), `langserver` now uses the name of the program of the last start for them, and the name of the server for the Output channel; the other packs have one program with the name of the server, so nothing changes for them.
963. **`python.languageServer: none` is `Config.Disabled`, a start that fails with `ServerDisabled`.** The host starts what the launcher answers, and a server with no program needs no launcher answer, so "none" is a launch that refuses on purpose: the item shows `$(circle-slash) <interpreter>: disabled`, nothing is told (it is what the user asked), and the setting is remembered by the start so that changing it restarts the server. The Output channel gets the host's line "Python cannot be started: Python is turned off: python.languageServer is none" once per start. New tag `ServerDisabled` (route tests in `langserver/launch_test.go`, `controller_test.go`).
964. **The interpreter is the host's choice.** "Select Interpreter" and `python.setInterpreter <path>` from a client of a hosted workspace (SPEC §10.18) answer an information notification and change nothing, the rule of decision 909 for the path of a server: the program runs on the host. `python.defaultInterpreterPath`, `basedpyright.path`, `pyright.path` and `pylsp.path` are `machine` settings, read from the user's settings only.
965. **The settings are written for Emetos, not copied from the extensions' `package.json` files.** 24b.2 copied rust-analyzer's list (197 settings, one third-party license). Pyright's, basedpyright's and python-lsp-server's lists are long and mostly the VS Code client's, and the three servers read different sections of `workspace/configuration` (verified against the pinned servers: Pyright asks for `python` and `pyright`; basedpyright for `python`, `basedpyright` and `pyright`; pylsp asks for nothing and takes `initializationOptions.pylsp`). The pack declares the ones that change what a server tells, 16 for basedpyright (`basedpyright.analysis.*`, four inlay hints and two switches), 12 for Pyright (`python.analysis.*`, `pyright.*`) and 33 for pylsp (`pylsp.plugins.*`, `configurationSources`, `signature.*`), with the names, types and defaults the servers document, and sends those that differ from the default. A setting a server has and the pack lacks is reachable through the project's own `pyrightconfig.json` or `pyproject.toml` (both are watched files), which is also where Pyright and basedpyright say a project's settings belong. Not registered: Pylance-only settings, `python.pythonPath` and `python.venvPath` (the pack gives the interpreter itself), `python.analysis.indexing` and the rest of the client's.
966. **The interpreter goes to each server in its own way.** Pyright and basedpyright get `pythonPath` in the `python` section (the log line "Setting pythonPath for service" confirms it), pylsp gets it as `plugins.jedi.environment` of its initialization options unless the user set that setting. The server is restarted when another interpreter is chosen (`Choose` restarts it) and when `python.defaultInterpreterPath` changes (the start reads it). The version of `basedpyright-langserver`, which has no `--version`, is asked of its sibling `basedpyright` (`VersionCheck.Exe`), and of `pyright` for `pyright-langserver`; no minimum version is set, because no feature of the pack needs one.
967. **Command ids and titles.** "Python: Select Interpreter" is `python.setInterpreter` (VS Code's), "Python: Restart Language Server" is `python.analysis.restartLanguageServer` (Pylance's and the Python extension's id for it), and "Python: Organize Imports" is `python.organizeImports` (VS Code has no such id: its Python extension sorts imports with isort as `python.sortImports`, which Emetos does not run). The command asks Pyright to run `pyright.organizeimports` and basedpyright `basedpyright.organizeimports` for the file (the server answers with `workspace/applyEdit`; the pinned Pyright 1.1.414 does not offer sorting the imports as a code action any more, which the first version of this step assumed and the real-server test showed wrong) and python-lsp-server for the code action `source.organizeImports`, which only a plugin offers, telling the user when it offers none. No default keybinding: Shift+Alt+O of the editor asks for the code action, so it sorts the imports for python-lsp-server with a plugin only; the command is the way for Pyright and basedpyright (a follow-up: have the editor's command fall back to the server's command).
968. **The tools of the real-server tests.** `scripts/tool-versions.txt` pins `python-tools` (a requirements file with the sha256 of every wheel for CPython 3.14 on x86_64 Linux: basedpyright 1.40.2, python-lsp-server 1.15.0 with pyflakes 3.2.0, jedi 0.20.1, black, and what they bring in, installed with `pip install --require-hashes --no-deps` into `/var/tmp/emetos-tools/venv`) and `pyright 1.1.414` as an npm package (its integrity is pinned; `npm install --ignore-scripts` into `/var/tmp/emetos-tools/node`, run by the system's node). Pyright's own pip wrapper downloads node into the user's cache folder at its first run, so the npm package is used. `scripts/fetch-tools.sh` got the kinds `venv` and `npm`. The wheels with C code (`black`, `ujson`, `pytokens`) are CPython 3.14's: another Python needs the file regenerated (a step of its own, as the header says).
969. **Not built.** "Find..." in the interpreter picker (the input box takes a path), conda/poetry/pipenv/hatch discovery beyond `CONDA_PREFIX` and the `python` programs of `PATH` (the picker's "Enter interpreter path..." reaches any of them), creating an environment, notebooks, `python.analysis` settings that are Pylance's, and running the server in each root of a monorepo (one server per tab, decision 903). The interpreter list is found when the picker opens and when the interpreter in use is looked for again (a chosen, a changed setting, an activation), not by a watcher: an environment made while Emetos runs appears at the next "Select Interpreter".

## Step 24b.5: TypeScript and JavaScript

980. **The TypeScript pack owns the server for both packs.** One server entry (`typescript`) serves `typescript`, `typescriptreact`, `javascript` and `javascriptreact`, as the spec says ("`extensions/typescript`, also serving `extensions/javascript`"); two entries would start two servers for one project and two status items. The consequence is that a tab that turns the TypeScript pack off loses the JavaScript features too, and that the `javascript.*` settings are declared by the TypeScript pack. The JavaScript pack keeps its grammars and has no code; it is named "JavaScript" (it was "JavaScript Language Basics") and the TypeScript pack "TypeScript", as the other packs went (decisions 928 and 947).
981. **The folder's own TypeScript is offered once and never taken; Emetos always names the TypeScript the server runs.** typescript-language-server has no TypeScript of its own: it runs the `tsserver.js` of a package, and when nothing names one it takes the `node_modules/typescript` of the folder before any other, which is code the folder brought (the reasoning of decision 961 for a Python environment; Emetos has no workspace trust, and a TypeScript plugin or a patched `tsserver.js` runs as the user). So the pack sends `initializationOptions.tsserver.path` at every start: the TypeScript beside the server (`require.resolve` from the server's real file, so a link in a `bin` folder is followed) or `typescript.tsdk`, and the folder's own only when the user chose it. When there is no global TypeScript, the start fails with `TypeScriptNotFound` (and names the folder's own) rather than letting the server pick it. The offer is a notification, once per activation, the first time a TypeScript or JavaScript file is in front; its two buttons are **Use Workspace Version** and **Select Another...**, and the choice (`global` or `workspace`) is kept per folder in the state (`typescript.version`, decision 960). `typescript.enablePromptUseWorkspaceTsdk` is VS Code's name and is **true** here (false in VS Code) for the same reason. Nothing is run to describe a version: the number is read from the package's `package.json`. A client of a hosted workspace cannot choose (decision 964's rule).
982. **The folder's `node_modules/.bin` is not searched for the server.** The spec's list had "the folder's `node_modules/.bin`, then `PATH`"; a program in the folder is the same kind of thing as its TypeScript, and a project that wants its own server can say so in `typescript.languageServer.path` (user settings only, `${workspaceFolder}` is expanded), which is an explicit choice. The SPEC bullet is changed. A server installed in `~/.npm-global/bin` or a version manager's folder is found when that folder is on `PATH`, as it is for the terminal; no other place is guessed.
983. **Two things the host needed.** typescript-language-server takes its per-language settings (`format`, `inlayHints`, the code lenses, `completeFunctionCalls`) only from `workspace/didChangeConfiguration`, and the host sent that notification only when a setting of the server's fixed `ConfigurationSection` changed, never at the start. `ServerLaunch.Settings` is sent right after `initialized`, before any document opens (nil sends nothing); the later servers of M24b (jdtls, FsAutoComplete, kotlin-lsp) take settings that way too. And a server that is a script run by a named node needs a program that is not the file that was found: `langserver.Launch.Command` replaces the program of the launch (the item still names the server that was found). Both are small, general and have tests in the host and in `langserver`.
984. **The settings are VS Code's names for what the server reads, written for Emetos.** About 110 settings from a table (`settings.go`): the classic `typescript.*` and `javascript.*` pairs, not the newer `js/ts.*` ones that VS Code's package.json now lists next to them (two names for one thing double the settings editor; the classic ones are what users have in their files). VS Code's defaults, except the offer above. The server takes the preferences (`quoteStyle`, import specifiers, the auto-import switches) for every file, so the value of `typescript.*` wins when it differs from its default and `javascript.*`'s is used otherwise; the settings of a language (format, inlay hints, code lenses) reach the server per language. `typescript.tsdk`, `typescript.nodePath`, `typescript.npm` and `typescript.languageServer.path` are `machine` settings (they decide what Emetos runs). `typescript.disableAutomaticTypeAcquisition` stays false as in VS Code: tsserver fetches `@types` packages with npm for a JavaScript project, which uses the network; the tests turn it off. `maxTsServerMemory` is always sent (3072, VS Code's). The tab size and indentation of the server's own edits are answered from `editor.tabSize` and `editor.insertSpaces` (a change restarts the server, because every setting the launcher reads does). Not registered, and listed in the SPEC: the file-move imports, workspace symbols, the tsserver log and plugins, the implicit project options, the client-only `suggest.*`.
985. **Organize Imports from the editor is general now.** TypeScript needs nothing: the server announces `source.organizeImports.ts`, which enables Shift+Alt+O (the real-server test checks it). The gap 24b.4 left for Pyright (decision 967) is closed with `extension.CodeActionKinds`, an optional interface of a `CodeActionProvider` that lists the kinds it answers: the editor enables Organize Imports from the kinds of the providers, and an extension's provider declared none, so a pack could not make the shortcut work. The adapter gives the list to the registry, which asks the provider only when a kind is wanted (a quick fix request no longer calls it). The Python pack uses it to offer "Organize Imports" for Pyright and basedpyright as an action that runs its own command (`python.organizeImports`, which asks the server for `pyright.organizeimports`); not for python-lsp-server, whose plugin offers the action itself. A limit: the command works on the file in front, so Organize Imports on save of a file that is not in front would sort the one that is; the editor saves the file in front in nearly every case, and a follow-up could pass the document as the command's argument.
986. **Node.** The server is a script with a `#!/usr/bin/env node` line. With nothing set, the first `node` on `PATH` must exist (otherwise the start fails with an error that says so, instead of the shell's "no such file" in the Output channel and five crashes); with `typescript.nodePath` Emetos runs `<node> <the script> --stdio` itself. No Node version is checked: the server prints its own message (6.0.2 needs 22.22.2).
987. **The commands.** VS Code's ids and titles; `javascript.goToProjectConfig`, `javascript.sortImports` and `javascript.removeUnusedImports` as VS Code has them, `typescript.organizeImports` once (VS Code has no id of its own for it: the spec names it). tsserver's `projectInfo` and `fileReferences` go through the server's command `typescript.tsserverRequest`, and the answer is shown with the pieces Emetos has: a document at a position, and a quick pick for several places (VS Code's file references go to its references view). "Go to Project Configuration" says what to add when the file is in no project and does not create the file. The source actions use `ApplyCodeAction`, so the edit is one undo step and the server's `workspace/applyEdit` is applied as every other.
988. **The tools of the tests.** `scripts/tool-versions.txt` pins typescript-language-server 6.0.2, typescript 6.0.3 (the last release with a `tsserver.js`; 7.0.2 is the native compiler and has none, which is why the install command says `typescript@6`) and `typescript5`, TypeScript 5.9.3 installed under another name with a sixth column in the file (`typescript5@npm:typescript@5.9.3`; `fetch-tools.sh` takes it as the thing to install). The tests link `typescript5` into the project's `node_modules` as `typescript`, so the folder's version differs from the one beside the server and the tests can tell which one ran. All three have their npm integrity pinned; the system's node (22.23.1) runs them.
989. **The real-server tests send the protocol the commands send; they do not run the commands.** The rig of `internal/gui/exthost` has no editor in front for `Documents().Active()` and no way to show a document, so the tests send the same `workspace/executeCommand`, `ApplyCodeAction` and code action requests and read the answers, and the commands themselves (what they ask, in which units, what they show) are tested over the fake API with the answers the real server was seen to give (`{"type":"response","body":{...}}` for `typescript.tsserverRequest`, a list of locations for the source definition). The fake API got `FailShow` to test a place that cannot be opened.
1000. **jdtls is found as a folder, and a `jdtls` script of a distribution is not run.** SPEC says `java.jdt.ls.path` (a folder) and then `jdtls` on `PATH`. The server is not one program: Emetos needs the launcher jar and `config_linux`, to run `java` itself with its own `-data` and `-configuration` (decision 1001). So a `jdtls` in `PATH` counts when its links lead to a `bin/jdtls` beside `plugins/` and `config_linux/` (the archive of the project, unpacked and linked), and a script that only starts the server from files elsewhere (a distribution's wrapper) is refused with a message that names it and says to set `java.jdt.ls.path`. The alternative was to run the wrapper with `-data`, which every distribution spells differently, and to lose control of the Java and the configuration folder. The places searched after `PATH` (`~/.local/share/jdtls`, where the install command puts the archive, and `/usr/share/java/jdtls`, `/usr/share/jdtls`, `/usr/lib/jdtls`, `/opt/jdtls`) are guesses about where distributions put the folder, none of them verified on a machine here; the setting is the sure way. The install help gives `sudo pacman -S jdtls` for Arch (Arch has a package of that name, which I could not check here) and, for every other system, a command that unpacks the latest snapshot of `download.eclipse.org/jdtls` into `~/.local/share/jdtls`; Fedora and Debian have no jdtls package that I know of.
